mr president, cleaner or dirtier, greener or greyer, the so-called 'energy union' means nothing but the great and liberalised single european energy market. the consequences will be no different from those in other liberalised sectors: monopolistic concentration on a european scale, benefiting energy oligopolies and harming consumers, especially the most vulnerable. for us, energy should be seen as a public good. the public, democratic control of the energy sector is therefore a prerequisite for ensuring its sustainability, with the necessary paradigm shift based on the high dependence on fossil fuels and on unsustainable energy and material flows to meet the basic needs of humanity, but also to ensure that no one is excluded from access to this essential good, by combating energy poverty. we are intervening in this process, arguing for the need for ambitious targets, both in terms of energy efficiency and renewable energy, in the context of respect for national sovereignty over this strategic sector and the necessary break with a production mode that is fundamentally unsustainable and irrational.

the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the environment is protected, and that the environment is protected in a way that is compatible with the objectives of the treaty. the seas provide food, energy, mobility, medicine and leisure, and are a source of economic growth. the oceans, together with coastal and marine resources, play a vital role in human well-being and economic and social development worldwide. according to oecd estimates, ocean-based industries represent a global gross value added of around eur 1.3 billion, which could double by 2030. oceans also host a rich, fragile and largely untapped biodiversity that provides a variety of important ecosystem services and resources. marine and coastal economies are developing around the world, but their success depends on better sustainability. the oceans also play a key role in regulating the climate system, supplying half of our oxygen, absorbing about 25 percent of the co2 emissions produced and redistributing heat across the planet. the oceans are actually our biggest allies against climate change and they were included in the paris agreement. however, our oceans are extremely vulnerable to overexploitation, climate change such as global ocean warming, environmental degradation, water acidification due to emissions from human activities, pollution and biodiversity loss. some of these most urgent global challenges, including climate change, poverty, safe and sufficient food for a population projected to reach 9 billion by 2050, can only be effectively addressed if the oceans are safe, clean and managed sustainably. addressing all these challenges requires better international governance in order to strengthen efforts at regional and global levels. these challenges have been recognised globally both at the rio+20 summit and in the un 2030 agenda for sustainable development, which has identified the conservation and sustainable use of our oceans as one of the 17 sustainable development goals and as part of a highly interconnected agenda. the 2016 joint communication of the european commission and the high representative on the international governance of the oceans, which is an integral part of the european union's response to the 2030 agenda, was based on a broad consensus on the need to strengthen the governance framework for the oceans, reduce pressures on them, exploit them sustainably and that this would require a greater knowledge of the oceans, proposing 50 actions to ensure safe, clean and sustainably managed oceans in europe and globally. international ocean governance is concerned with three areas of action for the european union: sustainable development, growth and employment and the union's intervention in the world. in this context, the maritime economy and international competitiveness are of particular importance to europe, given the increasingly worrying state of the oceans and the impact of human activities on them. in one case as in another, the success of management is a challenge to be met at world level. the union and the member states must act more firmly and together. the report, which will be voted on tomorrow, encourages the commission to develop partnerships in the field of oceans with the main players in this field as a means of achieving better international ocean governance. the way we deal with the oceans is crucial to our survival as a species and in malta, commissioner vella said at the our ocean conference that the oceans are the blue heart of the earth and that it is up to all of us to keep that heart beating. we now have the urgent task of acting in defence of the oceans and of creating a new and ambitious international governance of the oceans. and for all that, ladies and gentlemen, i hope i can count on your vote in favour tomorrow when we vote.

in writing. the eu is one of the most successful projects in the history of mankind: it has brought europe's longest period of peace, brought people closer together, promoted human rights and openness to the world, prosperity and convergence, the sharing of knowledge and the circulation of ideas, abolished borders and divisions. the complexities of this extraordinary supranational construction make any substantial change, however, a lengthy and sometimes tortuous process, filled with dead ends. this delay contrasts with the acceleration of time that characterizes today's world, which is confronted with profound changes in the technological, energy, economic and labour market sectors, among others, which are causing anxiety and insecurity among europeans. at the same time, internal fractures that have emerged or been discovered in recent years persist, although mitigated, and sovereigntist rhetoric has returned in force to the media space. despite all these difficulties, the historical balance of this political project can only be positive. the eu's great challenge is to stand firm in its assertion of the superiority of the values of freedom, openness, solidarity and moderation, refusing to succumb to closure, selfishness and extremism.

madam president, the youth employment initiative is based on a tripod of job creation, training and traineeships. and in my region, as in many member states, this tripod is unbalanced, because we are investing much more in training and traineeships than we are investing in the supply of jobs. and so we are not contributing to the fight against precariousness. we have a second problem with one of the tripod feet, which is precisely the formation. our young people are being thrown into cycles of continuing training, of training which is completely inadequate, of training which is completely inconsequential, when we have to ensure the opposite. we must ensure that our young people have individual support in multidisciplinary teams, made up of technicians, psychologists and specialist sociologists, to ensure that the training offered is appropriate both to the trainee's characteristics and to the needs of the companies. finally, traineeships cannot mask employment contracts nor can they replace employment contracts. and so it is necessary to ensure that the trainee's functional content and responsibility are properly defined and that he is accompanied by a supervisor who supports him and gives him training. more control is needed, with appropriate means of inspection. more transparency and a qualitative evaluation of the programme's results are needed. finally, the youth employment initiative must be consistent. this implies greater social mobility and that we reduce social inequalities in the european union.
madam president, commissioner, youth must be the priority of the european union. this has budgetary consequences, of course, and also national budgets, since the responsibility for employment is a national responsibility. the european union has done its part. member states should use the various funds they have for the promotion of employment. in cohesion policy alone, this amounts to 350 billion euros. the european social fund, the most important element for proper training, employment and education, has some 80 billion euros, and the member states must use it for this purpose. the european union has done its part and has, for example, increased the youth employment initiative by eur 116 million in the reconciliation for this year's budget. the youth employment initiative is an instrument that demonstrates the solidarity of the european union. it helps the poorest member states and regions, those with the highest youth unemployment, to improve their inclusion. it thus strengthens social and territorial cohesion. we need to strengthen, improve the youth employment initiative. we must continue this initiative. the european union needs inclusion, it needs territorial cohesion and, to return to the way i started, youth must be the priority of the european union and young people must be involved in the implementation of programmes and in the formulation of policies that concern them.

member of the commission. – madam president, i would like to thank the rapporteur and the honourable members for their support and for the interesting remarks and comments. your report is very timely as we are now in the process of elaborating our proposals for the future funding of youth employment under the next multiannual financial framework. fighting youth unemployment will continue to be a high priority at european and national levels as long as the rates of young unemployed and of those not in employment, education or training (neets) remain excessively high. the targeted funding and well targeted, as you know, targeted at specific regions and groups of young people, and we have specific results indicators that act as an additional driver of quality. the commission is also monitoring the quality of employment for all age groups under the european semester, with particular attention being paid to issues related to labour-market segmentation. we provide guidance on the in the employment committee (emco) multilateral surveillance process, and, as some of you might know, conclusions are expected soon – early this year. for the future, the commitment to quality offers in writing. the latest eurostat data confirm a situation of high structural unemployment in the european union and the euro area. systemic unemployment coexists with another reality which the statistics do not disguise: abnormally low wage growth, a compression of the price of labour, which takes advantage of the social pressure exerted by the reserve army of the unemployed to increase the exploitation of those who still find work. two realities that particularly affect the youngest and confirm that the capitalist crisis has been used to force entry into a new level of exploitation. the european union and its policies are active in promoting this race to the bottom in terms of rights, wages, living and working conditions. in portugal, youth unemployment is 25%. the youth employment initiative is a palliative. but what practice shows is its almost residual impact. we have repeatedly proposed an increase in the resources allocated to this programme and a clarification of its rules, with a view to preventing the promotion of forms of precarious work. significantly, these proposals have been rejected by the right and the social democrats in this parliament. who now come here crying crocodile tears for youth unemployment.
in writing. unemployment particularly affects the youngest. the european union and its policies are active in promoting this race to the bottom in terms of rights, wages, living and working conditions. in portugal, youth unemployment is 25%. this is the case in many other member states. the youth employment initiative does not solve the structural problems of unemployment. worse, it becomes a factor in devaluing work, whether in the bond, in the remuneration or in its flexibilization. in other words, young people, wherever they find work, training or training, become a stick in the mud. but still its macroeconomic impact is almost residual as the statistics show. we have repeatedly proposed an increase in the resources allocated to this programme and a clarification of its rules, with a view to preventing the promotion of forms of precarious work. significantly, these proposals have been rejected by the right and the social democrats in this parliament, who now come here crying crocodile tears over youth unemployment.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to begin by congratulating the commission on the launch of the first european strategy for plastics and on the efforts it is making to harmonize environmental protection and protection with economic growth and the innovation it brings. we all know that human society is very much dependent on plastic. over 70 percent of the products we use in our daily lives are made from composite materials made of plastics. the reality is that we europeans generate 25 million tonnes of plastic waste a year, of which less than 70% is collected for recycling, the rest ends up in our oceans, polluting everything in its path, endangering the very sustainability of the planet. the need to focus on recycling and design, mr vice-president, is not enough. we also need to change behaviours, to change the culture of disposable production and consumption, and to encourage the use of durable and reusable materials. ladies and gentlemen, it is an evidence that today's society is unfortunately plastic-dependent. so i think it's up to all of us to create the conditions for this dependence to end in favour of a more sustainable environment. i'm going to say, vice presidents, as vice president timmermans said, if we don't change the way we produce and use plastic items, by 2050, there will be more plastic than fish in our oceans.
madam president, the market and its sacralization have imposed unsustainable irrational practices and behaviour in the use of disposable goods, demonstrating a huge disregard and contempt for the very physical limits of the planet. it is essential that the production and use of waste and unnecessary products be reduced, but we are not going to do that with the market approaches advocated by the european commission or with so-called economic instruments. the application of the polluter-pays principle, which means reserving the right to pollute to those who can pay for it, does not make pollution less harmful because someone, in this case the consumer, pays for it. there is nothing to prevent the distribution, whether paid or unpaid, of unnecessary and non-biodegradable plastic bags from being simply put to an end. that the proliferation of unnecessary packaging be tackled and that packaging which can be reused by the distributor be encouraged by taking its cost off the consumer, and more, so that it no longer constitutes a cost, either economic or environmental. it's that in the waste management hierarchy, before recycling comes reduction.
mr president, we know that organised crime derives more income from human trafficking than from drug and arms trafficking, and is often associated with them, fuelling business and other criminal activities that money laundering and tax havens facilitate. we know that the object of trafficking in women is forced marriage and situations of clandestine and/or domestic work in conditions of slavery. but the key is sexual exploitation, prostitution. prostitution is a serious social scourge that enslaves millions of women, trapped in criminal networks of human trafficking and other forms of exploitation that some want to call voluntary prostitution or prostitution by choice of women. it is a serious attack on women, on their bodies, on their physical and psychological health, on their dignity and rights. prostitution feeds a sordid exploitation of women from the poorest classes and strata, constituting a serious violation of human rights. it is therefore urgent to reject any initiatives such as those which some in portugal are now trying to take to regulate prostitution, legitimising violence against women and the trafficking associated with it.
madam president, the regulation of professional qualifications strengthens the single market and labour mobility and thus strengthens the european union in practice. it should be remembered that the bologna process has brought about convergence in the level of academic qualifications, alongside the european qualifications framework, which allows, for example, a young portuguese graduate to have his degree recognised in any other european member state. but this convergence is incomplete. the biggest problem is the specialised professions which require recognition by professional bodies, which is why i am calling for the creation of a european framework of professional qualifications which harmonises the requirements of work. i also advocate, mr commissioner, that we strengthen european federations of professional bodies where they exist and that we create them where they do not exist, so that, for example, a young portuguese person who joins the association of psychologists in portugal will automatically be recognised as having this ability and will also be automatically recognised by the association of psychologists, for example, in the united kingdom, thus allowing effective job mobility also in the specialised professions.

mr president, the scandal that has resulted from the knowledge of the automobile emissions cheating has shed some light on the standards by which these multinationals are governed. the profit motive overrides the environment, health, ultimately, even human life, an eloquent portrait of capitalism. the european commission was not spared. the complacency or even complicity of those who knew that the tests carried out and the reference values could not be proved was evident in this process. the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter is a very serious matter. in practice, the rule is 'business as usual'. the revelations now made that several manufacturers have used human and other primate guinea pigs to test the health effects of diesel engine emissions confirm that we are not dealing with isolated episodes but rather with a systemic problem, and for systemic problems only systemic solutions.
mr president, trade, even in its so-called electronic form, is based on concrete production and distribution processes and on concrete social relations of production, real and not virtual, not dematerializable. the sale of goods and services in the single market, even by electronic means, expresses a link established through this market between the various producers, between workers in different countries, who are in direct competition, taking advantage of differences in wages and rights to force a general devaluation of their labour force. in a capitalist integration this is the meaning of the so-called free movement of goods and services: leveling down, increasing exploitation, conquering markets and increasing profits online or offline. it is obvious that the full potential of electronic commerce, as in general the enormous potential of scientific and technical acquisitions for the benefit of workers and consumers, has yet to be unlocked. it is true that there are obstacles in the online environment that frustrate consumers' natural expectations, but working to overcome them should not make us lose sight of this larger picture.
it is true that tax revenues are very important for the member states and it is true that there is a tax loss of millions of euros every year in relation to vat which could be spent on public services. all this is true, but there are several problems with this reform: the first is that we are currently witnessing this tendency of the european commission to act mainly in a transfer of the collection of direct taxes to indirect taxes. it should be the exact opposite, because only direct taxes ensure that there is a real redistribution. the second problem has to do with this definition of minimums. the definition of minimum rates does not eliminate the profound inequality in taxation at the level of the european union, and therefore taxation of capital income has been steadily declining in recent decades, while the burden of taxation on the working population has been increasing. and then there is the matter of substance, commissioner, and that is the end of it. it is pointless to talk about minimum rates as long as there are privileged tax jurisdictions in the european union, because there is a name for this: it is called tax evasion.
madam president, commissioner, the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions by at least 40% by 2030 is a key objective to ensure the sustainability of the planet, the quality of the air, but also to give a new impetus to the modernisation of the european economy. i welcome the work that has been done which has made an important contribution in terms of flexibility in the management of licences to improve the transparency and rationality of the market. efforts have also been made, although not with complete success, to reduce the distortions of competition caused by different national indirect cost policies. simplification factors were introduced and two major funds were set up to support the transition of industries, fostering innovation and modernisation, and protecting employment and competitiveness. we are therefore not dealing with the best, but with a reasonable proposal. efficiency and monitoring are needed for the decarbonisation and innovation objectives to be fully achieved.
madam president, there is a problem at the starting point in the approach to the objective of reducing greenhouse gas emissions, which is the choice and insistence on a market approach which has clearly revealed not only its ineffectiveness but also its perversity. from that initial resounding failure, it occurred to some good souls that by putting a few patches in the system, it would finally lead us to the stated goals. let it go. the creation of a trade in pollution licences has laid the foundations for yet another multimillion-dollar scheme to generate fictitious financial assets, perhaps another speculative bubble ready to be inflated. it has done nothing, and it is not doing anything, to achieve the desired reduction in greenhouse gases, especially if we want to achieve it within a framework of social justice and economic sustainability. what would have been a wonderful incentive to adopt low-carbon technologies has become the biggest disincentive. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council and parliament, and the council will be consulted on it.
madam president, in the global world we live in today, an economic power like the european union must be at the technological frontier in all sectors and in some of them must consistently take the lead. in view of its dependence on external supplies of fossil fuels and its commitment to digitisation and decarbonisation, the energy transition must be one of the sectors in which this leadership must be asserted and to which resources, talents and political determination must be channelled, not least because it calls for core knowledge such as mathematics, physics, chemistry, biology, social sciences, and promotes the integration between digitisation and decarbonisation. i therefore welcome this report for its timeliness and the richness of its content. the european union has been ahead, but has unfortunately been losing ground in the energy transition. in clean mobility it has even been overtaken by other industrial powers. let us therefore, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, make this report a mobilizing impulse in the service of citizens and future generations.

the multiannual financial framework (mff) is the fundamental instrument determining the budgetary policy of the european union. it sets annual expenditure ceilings for a minimum period of five years in line with the political priorities agreed by all member states. although we are still in the full 2014-2020 mff, the discussion on the next post-2020 mff has already started. the european commission will present a first proposal as early as may this year, and the debate has already begun. we hear a lot of calls for a budget that is tailored to the ambitions of this eu, but the truth is that the next mff has its contours already well defined. one need only read the latest strategic documents produced by the european commission, from the white paper on the future of europe to the reflection paper on the future of eu finances, to guess what the key direction of the next eu budget might be. in general terms, what is emerging is a mff with fewer resources, more geared to new priorities such as 'security and defence', with the arms race and its redistributive dimension reduced and increasingly subordinated to so-called conditionality.
in writing. female genital mutilation is a crime that cannot be justified in culture or tradition. it's a violation of the human rights of over 200 million women and girls worldwide. it is estimated that more than 500,000 women in europe have been victims of this practice, with more than 6,000 cases in portugal. we cannot accept these figures, which should urge us to act more decisively as another international day of zero tolerance for female genital mutilation approaches. if we do nothing, the number of victims will continue to rise and millions more women will see their lives affected by a barbaric practice that prevents their full assertion and undermines equality. in the european union there are measures we have to take. in the framework of the common european asylum system we must guarantee refuge to those who flee this barbaric practice. in development cooperation we must consider eradication policies. within europe, we must ensure that criminalisation is matched by more effective monitoring and more effective prevention and awareness-raising.
in writing. according to unicef, around 200 million girls worldwide have been victims of fgm, with 80 cases of female victims reported in portugal in 2016. female genital mutilation is one of the most sordid forms of violence against women, practiced in childhood, inflicting lifelong emotional damage and affecting the most vulnerable: girls up to the age of 15. it is a serious violation of their rights, causing infections, diseases, complications during childbirth and irreparable damage to their physical, sexual and psychological health, and may even lead to their death. it is important to set concrete objectives, measures and commitments for the eradication of female genital mutilation, with a strong focus on the sexual and reproductive health and rights of women worldwide. it is essential to criminalise and punish criminals, but also to prevent them. to take actions to raise awareness and awareness in the communities where these crimes are committed. educating new generations about women's rights. the fight to eradicate female genital mutilation is inseparable from the fight to protect and strengthen women's sexual and reproductive rights, access to education and, in particular, sexual education, and to improve maternal and child health, and to pursue a path of progress and social justice.

the rapporteur. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the report on the composition of parliament, which i am presenting with my colleague danuta hübner, is intended to achieve what has never been done before: to ensure a parliament with fairer representation and at the same time to ensure that no country loses seats. this sounds simple, but it's never been done. whenever it has been necessary in the past to adjust the composition of parliament, it has been necessary for some to lose so that others can gain. and we know why it's different now: we are discussing the re-composition of parliament in the context of brexit and therefore have the opportunity to allocate at least some of the uk seats. of course, in the past, tensions between winners and losers have always prevented deviations from the status quo and led to pragmatic solutions where possible political compromises have always fallen short of a fairer representation. none of that is happening now in this proposal, because we have the fantastic opportunity to finally have a fairer parliament that more faithfully represents the population of the member states and at the same time ensures that no member state loses seats in parliament. this is now indeed possible and we must not lose this opportunity. the redistribution of uk seats must be limited, of course, because the people would not understand that a large country leaves the european union and the size of the parliament is not reduced. but some distribution is necessary if we do not want to lose this opportunity. and so, after listening carefully to the experts, after listening to this parliament, starting with the shadow rapporteurs, the proposal that my colleague danuta hübner and i put forward is this: first, that all countries maintain the number of members they have now. it's, i would say, the basic principle, no one loses seats. secondly, that in addition 27 of the 73 seats in the united kingdom which will be left vacant be distributed among those states which are currently under-represented. it is a fair, balanced, politically viable proposal which has already been approved by the committee on constitutional affairs and which i hope will be approved today by this house. our key to the division is not the application of any of the many mathematical formulas that have been discussed and that have not achieved political or technical consensus. but it is not a simple pragmatic solution like the one that has been in force until now. what we are proposing is a solution based on principles, the principle of degressive proportionality and the principle that no state loses seats. by redistributing only 27 of the 73 seats, we are naturally also proposing a reduction in the parliament and leaving a margin of 46 seats available for future enlargements or for the eventual creation of transnational lists. and let me say just a few words about the transnational lists. and the first is to clear up a misunderstanding. this report is about the composition of parliament, not about the transnational lists. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the new regulation to include the areas of the environment, transport, energy, transport and energy, and the environment. and that is why the report says that this council decision is a precondition. that is what is at stake and no more than that. i hope that no point of disagreement will prevent this parliament from sending a clear message to the council and proposing a parliament ... (the president cut the speaker off)
mr president, we fully support the composition and allocation of seats designed by the co—rapporteurs, but we are very worried about the transnational list political recommendations in this report. we have managed to build a sound and trustworthy democracy here in the european union, where there is an equilibrium and balance between majorities and states. if we accept the constitutional frankenstein monster that is the transnational list, we accept something that does not exist in any federal state, as mr verhofstadt has proved here today because he cannot give one example of it, not even in belgium that has quite a similar system. if we are not a federation, why should we have a joint constituency that not even federations have? do we really need, in order to build a democracy in the european union, to have a top—down elite selected and co-opted by european parties, or – what is really very dangerous – put forward by european movements of national parties that can divide, even in regional terms, the european union’s public sphere? please think about it and vote against the transnational lists paragraph. (applause)
mr president, what is at stake in this debate is much more than the so-called transnational lists, an artificial creation based on a federalist vision that has nothing to do with the reality of europe and that is contrary to the need for a europe of cooperation between sovereign and equal states. but they do not want to make the transnational lists a screen to hide everything else. and everything else is a proposal for the composition of the european parliament which prolongs existing imbalances, which reduces the possibilities of a more faithful representation, because it is more plural, of the will of each people, which aggravates injustices by renouncing the possibility of compensating, as was possible and fair, some member states for the loss of members during enlargements. the amendments we have tabled to the report are intended to correct these imbalances and the unjustified discrimination against countries like portugal. if we resume the debate on a fairer basis, and even within the rules laid down in the treaty, we can reach a fair and acceptable proposal, which is neither fair nor acceptable.
he asks under the blue card procedure. madam president, briefly, just to ask how the quality of democracy in the european union is strengthened by transnational lists where only the representatives of a few countries are elected, invariably the largest which, because of the human nature and the size of the countries, therefore control the essential political groups. in a transnational list, the first of these lists will be elected. do you really believe that the representatives of the small and mediumsized countries of the european union will be at the top of these lists? that's the question i'll leave you with.
madam president, the european parliament is the only european body, the only institution that is directly elected by the citizens and on a proportional basis, and i believe that it should remain so. we already have a sufficient disproportion in relation to the other european institutions, in particular in relation to the european council, where six countries hold 70% of the votes, and that is why i am not in favour of transnational lists. i don't think they add democracy, i think they subtract democracy. but i must say, madam president, that as far as the composition of parliament is concerned, the proposal put forward by the rapporteurs is a serious one and the fairest of all those we have seen so far. it is the only proposal in which small and mediumsized countries do not lose representation, and i know that it is very popular to say that we should all win and that our countries should be more represented, but i also know that, in saying this, we would have to have a parliament far above the limit set in the treaties and that obviously someone would have to be removed, only it is not said who will be removed. it's not satisfactory. madam president, i would first of all like to congratulate the rapporteurs on the fairness with which they have distributed the seats among the various member states. the principle of proportionality in reverse has finally been fully realised. but this relative justice, which has been achieved here, will always remain incomplete if we are not able to take the decisive step towards greater integration at this stage of the european construction process and to really get the union's institutions to discuss european problems in the election campaigns. the election campaigns in the various member states for the european parliament are centred around national problems, they are a remake of national elections. we must therefore take the decisive step of discussing europe in the elections to the european parliament. that this parliament should be the representative of the european people and not of national achievements.
the rapporteur. madam president, two points mark this debate. first, as one would expect, the discussion on transnational lists, but i would like to stress that what this report does is to leave a set of places available which can be used in the future, either in enlargements or in the eventual creation of transnational lists. the commission has also taken into account the fact that the commission has not yet adopted a decision on the establishment of a transnational electoral list. more importantly, this debate has revealed a very broad consensus on the rapporteurs' proposal for the distribution of seats, and that is very important. i believe there is good reason for very broad support for this proposal, not only from those who see their unfair under-representation finally corrected, but also from those small and medium-sized states which have gone into this debate fearing that they will lose seats once again and who see their representation threefold guaranteed. firstly, because the proposal does not reduce seats to any member state, secondly, because it does not enshrine any mathematical formula which implies a loss in the future, and thirdly, because it guarantees that even in the event of enlargement and even in the event of transnational lists, this distribution of seats is not affected. i hope therefore that we can get a clear message from this parliament to the council for a proposal which will allow for a fairer parliament and strengthen european democracy.
the report setting out the composition of the european parliament following the withdrawal of the united kingdom sets out an appropriate model which makes it possible to correct certain imbalances in representation, to ensure that no member state loses seats and to leave room for possible future enlargements or for the implementation of the principle of transnational lists, which was previously approved by parliament. i would like to stress the excellent work done by the rapporteurs and those who worked with them most directly, who, among other things, have managed to ensure that no country loses representatives, a fact which, if it did happen, would be very difficult to explain to national public opinion in the context of the freeing up of seats resulting from the departure of british members. the report also stresses the exclusive competence of the council to promote, if it so decides and unanimously, the legal framework for the introduction of transnational lists. i believe that these can be a positive step towards deepening european democracy, provided that distortions in the democratic representation of the various member states and of the various political groups and movements within the institutional framework of the union are avoided.

in writing. in many parts of the world - and not just in developing countries - we are witnessing authoritarian phenomena against democracy and human rights, often manifested in the reduction of the space for civil society to intervene. certain groups such as women, young people, lgbti people and minorities, roma communities, indigenous peoples, people with disabilities, the press or human rights defenders are preferred targets of repression, limitation of the rights to freedom of expression, assembly and association. the eu and its member states have a duty to lead by example in scrupulously respecting the fundamental rights of civil society and to counter any negative trends in this area. but the eu also has a duty to act when these fundamental rights are not respected in third countries with which it has commercial or diplomatic relations, using its external policy instruments, notably human rights and development instruments and bilateral agreements with third countries.

madam president, madam commissioner, summertime was first introduced over 100 years ago. at the time, it brought two great benefits: the enjoyment of the sunny day, synchronizing work with leisure, and the savings on the energy bill, which today is only marginal, as we all know. at first sight it seems that this resolution is only concerned with the inconvenience of having to change the timetable, but this inconvenience has to be assessed accurately, since all the evidence gathered so far seems to be inconclusive. in health, we should consider, on the one hand, the adaptation to a one-hour difference in the circadian rhythm and, on the other hand, the gains we have from increased sun exposure in diseases such as depression, osteoporosis, cardiovascular disease and respiratory diseases. however, whatever decision we take, we must act with caution and responsibility when we ask to discontinue the biannual clock. we must not forget, however, that if this decision can be left to the individual member states, as it may be and as we risk, we will create a schedule that is even more difficult to manage. on the other hand, there are not three time zones, there are four time zones in the european union. let's not forget the azores.

madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, in 2011, thousands of demonstrators in the symbolic tahrir square in cairo showed the world their will to fight for democracy and respect for human rights. seven years later, virtually nothing remains of the optimism of those days. a large-scale repressive atmosphere is being experienced in the run-up to presidential elections, which are scheduled for the end of march, and there is persecution of lgbti and human rights activists and journalists. there is an unprecedented number of enforced disappearances, confessions obtained under torture, arbitrary trials and, most seriously of all, an alarming increase in death sentences and executions, especially since the coming to power of president el sisi. under the pretext of fighting terrorism, the courts handed down 186 death sentences in 2017, three times the number imposed in 2016, and last january alone, 22 civilians were hanged after being sentenced to death by military courts. the european union, which is egypt's main economic partner, must urge the egyptian authorities to comply with their international human rights obligations, strongly condemn the use of the death penalty in that country and call for a moratorium on all impending executions.
madam president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, in haiti between 150 and 500 000 children between the ages of 7 and 14, the vast majority of them girls, are lent by their parents to families who employ them in domestic work, in conditions of virtual servitude. they are prevented from going to school, overworked, subjected to physical, verbal and sexual violence, and often victims of international child trafficking and prostitution networks. it is a sad irony that the country where the first abolition of slavery in history took place, and the only one whose independence began a slave revolt, maintains in the 21st century the tradition of the restavek. this authentic form of modern slavery is still culturally acceptable today thanks to the extreme poverty of the population, especially the rural population. the absence of social services and free public schools has been further exacerbated by the devastating 2010 earthquake and subsequent natural disasters. the european union must condemn this shameful situation and urge the haitian authorities to bring their domestic legislation into line with international obligations on human rights and the rights of the child and to put an end to the impunity with which child trafficking networks operate in that country.

the international criminal tribunal for the former yugoslavia closed on 31 december 2017, 24 years after its establishment. the trial of genocide, crimes against humanity, war crimes and violations of the geneva conventions was a complex exercise, with 161 indictments (of which 90 convictions, 13 referrals to national jurisdiction, 37 deaths or withdrawal of indictment and 19 acquittals), 4 650 witnesses, 10 800 trial days and 2 and a half million pages of case law. i note the european union's continued cooperation in the proceedings, by freezing assets or banning the movement of accused persons in its member states, and recall the requirement for cooperation with the icty in the accession agreement with croatia. i would therefore argue that any negotiation with serbia must take this matter into account. the court's contribution to international criminal law is undeniable and i hope that the case law produced will be useful for future similar mechanisms. unfortunately, we are witnessing today war crimes and flagrant violations of international humanitarian law, with syria in the foreground. i hope that the deepening of the principles of individual criminal responsibility in war situations will contribute to future determined action against the criminals who are already exterminating entire populations.

in writing. the year 2017 saw a familiar story repeat itself: once again, taxpayers were forced to pay ransom to private banks. the rescue of italian banks involved €17 billion of public funds. this money, taken directly from the pockets of the workers, was used to save two failing banks and protect their bondholders. in portugal, taxpayers' money continues to pay for the decisions of the bpn, banif or bes. but the much-hyped banking union, served to the people sweetened by the false promise of an end to bailouts made with taxpayers' money, actually means much more than the continuation of these same bailouts. we are also witnessing a wave of mergers and the drive towards monopolistic concentration of the financial sector on a european scale, with the creation of giant financial groups, all of them controlled by large groups, in the case of portugal all foreign. that is, all the opposite of what was announced with the banking union, supposedly created to end the "too big to fail" banks and end the regaboof of public funds to pay for the consequences of speculation and financial irresponsibility. only public control of banking can put the financial system at the service of the economy and development.
mr president, the tax evasion and money laundering schemes highlighted by the panama papers and paradise papers, among other scandals, show how there is a cross-border web of intermediaries, including consultants, bankers, lawyers, accountants and other service providers who have specialised in designing tax evasion and laundering schemes for large multinationals, for wealthy clients and for mafias, to avoid taxes and other legal obligations with opacity and impunity. that is why this commission proposal, which follows parliament's call in several reports, is so important. it aims to ensure that crossborder schemes designed by tax intermediaries or taxpayers are reported to the tax authorities of each member state, including information collected in the automatic exchange of information between tax authorities in the european union. the excellent report by emmanuel maurel, which will be adopted today, is intended to strengthen the commission by parliament. let us hope that governments in the council will not reduce the scope of reporting obligations and the sharing of information collected so that we can adopt robust legislation without subterfuge against transparency and accountability.

on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, this european council is taking place at a very special moment, where we have polls around the world coming up with a nationalistic tone in russia, china and the united states. in this context, the european union needs to assert its own vision and its own way of life and to have a long—term strategy, which should be based on human dignity, fairness, democracy and a clear commitment to sustainable development. yes, climate change is there, and we need to have a growth strategy driven by sustainable development goals. yes, the digital revolution can change our way of life, but we want to make the best of this by framing this revolution. that is why our commitment to the european pillar of social rights is so crucial. we need to tackle not only the old inequalities, but also the new ones. we are now waiting for the final content of the social fairness package to be adopted by the commission because we need to make sure that all citizens can have access to full coverage via social protection. but can we say that all citizens now have real access to all these benefits? this is not the case. we still have many inequalities across the union, and that is why we in the european parliament really expect a forward—looking community budget – the financial framework – to ensure that these promises will be delivered. the same applies to the eurozone. we need to stop the trend for divergence. that is why we expect the commission’s proposed budget line to build up fiscal capacity for the eurozone. but then we come to the big problem. how can we finance this? that is where the discussion on taxation, which will take place in this european council, is so crucial. let me give a very simple figure. when it comes to large corporations, something very weird is happening. in 2000, their profits were taxed at around 34%; now their profits are taxed at just 24%. so the situation we have is that large corporations are paying less and less tax, while our governments are under pressure to make cuts to welfare and healthcare systems. something is wrong, and we should not be surprised that citizens are now feeling insecure and that nationalistic and xenophobic positions are gaining ground. we need to reverse this trend. the only way to reverse this trend is for the european council to say, ‘if we want everybody to benefit from this strategy and for the social pillar to be implemented, we need to have the means’, and the means will be fair taxation. if the european council really wants to change the situation in europe, it needs to be bold on fair taxation. prime minister of portugal mr president, i would like to begin by thanking you for the many and varied questions and words of encouragement regarding the continuation of a policy which has not only allowed us to turn the page on austerity, but has also allowed us to achieve good results: good results in consolidating public finances, a deficit which last year was close to 1.1% with the start of the reduction of public debt, at the same time as we had the highest growth since the beginning of the century, we have an unemployment rate which has been falling steadily and is currently below 8%. your words encourage us and give us the energy to continue the success and the results of these policies. i would like to stress one important point here. the change in policy was not only of economic importance. it has also shown that we are not condemned to a single thought within europe and that we are free to develop political alternatives. this is fundamental to strengthening democracy and citizens' confidence. if today euroscepticism in portugal has fallen again, it is largely because the portuguese have understood that it is possible to be in europe in different ways. mr joão ferreira raised a question which has to do with the difficulties that the euro poses for countries like portugal. we know, at least since monetary union in the united states, that monetary unions do not reduce asymmetries, but rather accentuate them. all the preparatory studies for the euro, from the mclaughlin report to the delors report of 1989, made it clear that in order for the euro not to have an asymmetrical effect, it was necessary to provide the euro area with a budgetary capacity. two years ago, some people thought we couldn't get out of austerity without getting out of the euro, and others thought we couldn't get into the euro by getting out of austerity. today we know that it is possible to be in the euro without austerity. but what we also know is that all those who said that budgetary capacity was needed were right, and they are still right, and that is what i hope. the only way to ensure that there is a real budgetary capacity is to have a budgetary capacity. what is not possible is to come to each of our countries and say that we want more money from europe and then at the same time say that we want to give less money to europe. europe does not produce money for itself. europe's money comes from europeans and it comes in one of two ways: either because the member states increase transfers at the expense of their nationals' taxes, or we increase the european union's own resources by taxing the whole european economy. or we can find the right balance between these two sources of funding. i want the next budget to be a more robust budget and for that you must have more resources. i am therefore not afraid to say that portugal is ready to give more money to the european union. but we also support the idea that, in addition to the reinforcement that is being made at the expense of portuguese taxes, there should be new resources at european level, which, incidentally, will only be effective if they exist at european level. or will any country be able to effectively tax american digital multinationals if we cannot do it on a european scale? will we be able to tax environmentally harmful products better at european level or at national level? will we or will we not have the courage to pass the financial transaction tax? we must be courageous and we must tax, and that is the truthful speech we must make before our citizens. migration: schengen is a major 'acquis' of our union. it was schengen that gave freedom of movement within the european union. twelve years ago, as minister of the interior, i prepared, under the portuguese presidency, the enlargement of schengen to include the then new member states, and i am very proud of the awards i received, from poland to the czech republic, for having extended schengen to the new member states, restoring the freedom of movement that these peoples had not had for decades, because they were locked behind the iron curtain. it is this heritage of freedom of movement that we must be able to defend and that we must be able to continue to affirm. i am asked whether europe should be built at two speeds or, as i prefer to say, in variable geometry. no, i'm not going. as far as possible, we must continue to build the europe of 28 or 27, but we must respect the freedom of those who do not want to do what others want to do. there is no way for us to stay united without respecting freedom. but the freedom of those who do not want to go further cannot limit the freedom of those who want to go faster. so as long as this variable geometry is neither exclusive nor definitive and always keeps the door open for anyone who wants to join - as with the euro, as with schengen - there is no reason for us to remain paralysed, just because some do not want to keep up, and we must respect that freedom too. energy is a key issue. in portugal, we have already achieved 60% of production with renewable energy and 30% of consumption with renewable energy. we are well aware that we have natural conditions that others do not have. but in order to share these natural conditions, it is essential that we do not delay the process of energy interconnections any further. we must connect the iberian peninsula to the centre of europe, we must connect france, from france to germany, and the whole of europe, so that the whole of europe can benefit from the excess capacity for the production of renewable energy that naturally exists on the iberian peninsula and that can hardly exist, for purely natural reasons, in other regions of europe. i would like to end with two specific questions. first, with regard to fires. i would like to thank mrs miranda for speaking in a magnificent portuguese of galician inspiration, and since i cannot reply in galician, i will reply in portuguese, and i will say the following: the commission has already put forward steps to improve the european civil protection mechanism. the existence of a european reserve to supplement the national responsibility of each member state to be able to respond in the event of a tragedy is absolutely essential. but more important, or at least as important, is that we can act on the causes of the fires, and what we need is that the next common agricultural policy also takes into account the need to adapt the forest in southern europe to the new climatic conditions and to make it possible mr president, madam president-in-office, commissioner king, cybercrime has gone from being a risk to a reality with an increasing impact on our lives and the functioning of our communities. from scheduling a doctor's appointment to monitoring the electricity distribution network, we live in an interconnected city which requires specific action, both at european and national level, both in the public and private sectors, both in prevention and in combating it. we advocate, and in some cases have already approved, the strengthening of the agency for network and information security (enisa), the strengthening of europol's powers and resources, increased funding and close cooperation with private actors such as facebook and google. in this way, we have been equipping the union and the member states for this new reality. but we also need to adapt our means of proof, i.e. to ensure that the portuguese police have access to evidence that is on a server in ireland, even though the crime under investigation was against a portuguese citizen. this example makes it clear that the absence of borders in the digital world urgently requires the creation of a european approach to digital proof. that is why i am calling today on the european commission to come forward with a legislative proposal to regulate digital evidence, allowing for more effective combat and certainly greater safeguards in the defence of european rights.

the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, we are here today to discuss a new debate. this is certainly what has attracted so much attention and so many members. this parliament is called upon to discuss and decide on a proposal to terminate a fisheries agreement in force with a third country. bilateral fisheries relations between the european union and the comoros date back to 1988, i.e. for three decades the european union has had some kind of fisheries agreement with the comoros, which gives it access to the comorian fishery resources. the comoros is among the least developed countries. the fisheries sector, the second most important in the country, after agriculture, accounts for a significant share of both gdp and employment. all the domestic operations are small-scale and artisanal, and involved, not long ago, some eight thousand fishermen. the stocks that can be exploited in the comorian eez are mainly large pelagic fish, tuna and swordfish. the annual catches by local fishermen represent less than half of the estimated stocks. the remaining fish quantities are exploited by foreign industrial fleets, which land them elsewhere, and fish processing is also carried out outside the comoros, including catches, in some cases, of the local fleet. this is a framework we must bear in mind when we consider the european commission's proposal to terminate the fisheries agreement between the european union and the comoros. the comoros has also requested the commission to provide information on the fishing activities of the comoros in the waters of the comoros, including the fishing activities of the comoros in the waters of the comoros, and the fishing activities of the comoros in the waters of the comoros. we believe, however, that we must draw the attention of the european commission to certain issues by means of the resolution we are discussing here today. we believe that the fight against iuu fishing should not be limited to the identification of non-cooperating third countries. an effective fight against all forms of iuu fishing requires finding ways to help countries, in particular small island developing states, such as the comoros, to improve their marine management policies. in this respect, it is regrettable that, over the almost 30 years that the european union has had fisheries agreements with the comoros, no more tangible results have been achieved in the development of the comorian fisheries sector, in particular in terms of control and surveillance capacity, scientific development and technical training of fishermen and observers, among other areas. it is also with great concern that we note that assessments by un bodies criticise what they call the european union's position of power in setting fisheries agreements and fixing fish prices, while at the same time pointing out that the partnership agreements have failed to support the development of local industry, which is also evident. this situation justifies the presentation of proposals in the context of this resolution which could contribute to the continuation of support for the development of the fishing sector in the comoros, to the improvement of fishing activities, to the improvement of the quality of life of fishermen and, thus, to the fight against iuu fishing. these and other concerns are expressed in the resolution we are debating today and voting on tomorrow.
i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report, which is a very good report and i hope that the commission will be able to support it. it's the appropriate decision in the context of the files i've been given to review. but having said that, i would remind you that this small island state in the indian ocean is considered one of the least developed countries in the world and that its economy is very much dependent on financial and technical assistance from other countries. the fight against iuu fishing is a very appropriate priority for the european union. last year's fao report reveals that illegal fishing is on the rise in east africa, causing losses of €325 million in landings and around €800 million in processed products. the meeting of acp fisheries ministers in the bahamas last september recognised the enormous economic and environmental losses due to illegal, unregulated and unreported fishing. but denouncing agreements, although inevitable, is not the solution to ending these practices. following the implementation of the card system, the termination of the agreements and the inclusion of the countries in the list of iuu countries, the european commission should intensify cooperation efforts on comprehensive reforms. in addition, the comoros is home to a large population of coelacanths, a relic of biodiversity and of world value, a living fossil threatened by illegal fishing in that region of the indian ocean. i would stress the need for the commission to promote intensively aid for the development of fisheries management and governance and marine biodiversity in the comoros. a history of almost 30 years of agreements between the european union and the comoros, with no visible improvement in activity, shows that something has failed and that we have also failed. the fact that the agreement is now being withdrawn should therefore be an incentive to improve, as far as possible, the monitoring of cooperation for the development of this archipelago, which was said to have been discovered by the portuguese in 1505 and then colonized and administered by france for several centuries.
member of the commission. – mr president, the denunciation of this fisheries partnership agreement is, sadly, a first for the eu under the implementation of the regulation combating illegal, unreported and undocumented fishing (iuu) and measures forcing therein. this is a legal obligation, and it shows that the commission is looking globally at its external policy. the eu cannot continue with fisheries agreements when third-country partners do not take effective action to combat iuu fishing. the decision is fully consistent with the eu’s ocean governance agenda under which the union cooperates with third countries with the purpose of improving compliance with international measures, including on combating iuu fishing.
i would like to thank everyone for their contributions and to stress a final message to the european commission, which i think is clear from the various speeches. this denunciation should not mean the end of a cooperation relationship with the comoros in the field of fisheries, which is why we urge the european commission to work towards restoring this relationship as soon as possible, considering fishing communities and small-scale artisanal fisheries as a central factor in the development of this cooperation, promoting investments and technical assistance in areas as diverse as the fisheries management and governance system, monitoring, coast protection, surveillance and surveillance capacities, the activation of support structures for the refrigeration, distribution and processing of fish, the construction of landing and safety infrastructure, the renewal of the small-scale fleet, strengthening safety conditions and the ability to stay at sea, among many other aspects. in our view, the sustainable fisheries agreements concluded by the european union with other countries should be mutually beneficial. the strengthening of the sovereignty of these countries over their resources, the development of economic activities associated with fishing, the protection of marine resources and the promotion of the development of fishing communities must be the central objectives of this cooperation relationship, and not, as has also been pointed out here, the depletion of resources. if we go in this direction, it will also be an effective way of combating iuu fishing.

first vice-president of the commission. – mr president, this week marks a hugely important moment for the circular economy in europe. the new eu waste legislation that we are discussing here this evening will help to prevent waste and will turn much more waste into valuable resources. i trust that this house will vote clearly in favour of the adoption of the package on wednesday. in doing so, you would give a great push to further improving waste management in europe, in line with our circular economy goals. this will unlock new sources of growth and innovation, create jobs and reduce our dependence on imports of critical raw materials and our reliance on waste treatment abroad, and it will of course help to protect our environment and thus the health of our citizens. i do know that this house had its doubts when this commission in december 2014 withdrew previous proposals on waste. we did it to pursue more ambitious, better designed goals that would get the approval of member states and would be actually enforced on the ground. i would like to express my profound gratitude to the rapporteur, ms simona bonafè, all the shadow rapporteurs as well as all the other members of the european parliament and staff members involved for their relentless and constructive work on the commission’s new proposals over the past two years. and i do agree with the much broader framework mr gambús has just given us of why we are doing this. without your vision and support, we would not have achieved the very good result we have on the table today or indeed overcome the stalemate that the pre-2014 proposals had engendered. thanks to your support, the ambitious recycling and landfill reduction targets proposed by the commission were generally kept, even though member states may take some more years to attain them. parliament’s support was also crucial to agree on strong new rules on waste prevention, including food waste and marine litter, and you also helped to secure strong new rules on extended producer responsibility, the management of hazardous wastes and bio-waste, and the separate collection of waste. finally, you also pushed very hard for an ambitious waste policy agenda during the coming years. this agenda encompasses and reviews the many different pieces of existing eu legislation, ranging from reusability and recyclability of packaging waste, end-of-life vehicles, waste oils, food waste and waste disposal operations, as well as recycling targets for waste other than municipal and packaging waste. not all of parliament’s amendments found their way into the final text. i am aware, for instance, that you would have wanted to see an even stricter harmonisation of the calculation rules for recycling. unfortunately, the council could not agree on a single measurement point, yet the new legislation still marks a major step forward by ensuring that only waste that enters recycling will be reported under the targets. over the next years, the commission will closely monitor the implementation of these new calculation rules to ensure that only input to recycling is reported. if appropriate, the commission will consider if further harmonisation and calculating recycling targets is feasible. i should finally mention that among the commission declarations that accompany the package are four declarations responding to points that were particularly important to this house: first, on the policy framework for the circular economy; second, on the collaborative economy; third on microplastics; and fourth, on the reporting of waste data in 2020. our shared task now is to ensure that the promises enshrined in this waste package are actually delivered on the ground. this requires member states to urgently step up action, building on the progress that many of them are already making to meet existing obligations. local and regional administrations and businesses will also have a key role to play. your support in keeping up the pressure for full and effective implementation will be essential. the commission will continue to support member states in making the best use of available instruments in terms of new legislation, economic instruments and financial support. we will also provide guidance as requested on many new key provisions, for instance on how to modulate producer fees under extended producer responsibility schemes to promote more circular products. before concluding, i would like to very briefly touch on the other file in this joint debate – the standard reporting directive. repealing the existing directive is an important first step towards further administrative simplification and higher levels of legal certainty. the commission has actively pursued the follow-up to the fitness check on streamlining environmental reporting which aims at increasing transparency, optimising the use of it tools and reducing the administrative burden for all stakeholders. before the end of may, we will present a legislative proposal to amend the reporting requirements in a number of environmental directives and regulations modelled on the proposals for the revision of the drinking water directive which you are currently discussing. i believe that there is every opportunity to reach an agreement at first reading on this proposal too, so that the benefits can be delivered already in 2019, before the end of this legislature, and we have something to show for ourselves when we head for elections. we can all be truly proud – and you, especially, can be very proud – of this legislative package. once properly implemented, the new eu waste legislation will make the european union the world leader in waste management. the european parliament has played an essential role in laying a foundation for this transformation and i sincerely hope you will all lend your support to make it happen later this week.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has also adopted a number of amendments. mr president, commissioner, we agree with more ambitious targets for the recycling of packaging waste, but we must not lose sight of some issues. in accordance with the principle of the waste hierarchy, prevention, reduction of packaging waste production and reuse of packaging, in that order, should take precedence over recycling. extended producer responsibility schemes are appropriate in pursuing these objectives. the disparity in starting points between member states with regard to upwardly revised recycling and preparation for use targets means that different efforts are needed to achieve the targets set. we do not think that this has been taken into account. the existence of new objectives, revised upwards, justifies the existence of new means to achieve them. it would therefore be justified for the european union to strengthen the means at the disposal of the member states to support them in the necessary investments, in particular those that are lagging behind in the field of waste management infrastructure. but it happens that it is not happening.
the european union (eu) is the most import-dependent area of the world. it also leads the global commitment to the sustainability of the planet and the fight against climate change. the circular economy is a necessary complement to the energy transition. only through the implementation of this package will the eu be able to achieve levels of sustainability and competitiveness that will enable it to stand at the technological and social frontier of a changing world. the current model of resource management is economically, socially and environmentally unsustainable. you have to take the opportunity to change. creating wealth, creating jobs, creating a better quality of life for people. as one of the shadow rapporteurs, i advocated and proposed amendments to phase out landfilling to total elimination, to ban landfilling of waste that is subject to separate collection, to create a framework programme with a timetable of objectives, to monitor waste transfers within the eu and to third countries and to improve the quantification methodologies associated with landfilling. it's time for action. the circular economy has to be much more than a good narrative. it must be a concrete reality with positive impacts on the daily lives of europeans.
