mr president, we agree that the european union needs to diversify its crops and combat its structural protein deficit. now we also need to clarify that this dependence, these massive imports of soybeans, which are growing exponentially, have a source and that source is our intensive production model which is highly dependent on soybeans and compound feed, and therefore a sustainable strategy that seeks to combat this issue must of course take into account the need to revise this model. in short, i would say that to try to create a sustainable strategy to combat our protein dependence while pursuing a free trade policy with third countries is hypocritical, because it puts our producers in a situation of inequality which naturally pushes them towards this unsustainable consumption of imported protein.
mr president, we are following the principles underlying social and territorial cohesion. the facts now show that the european union has diverged, the gap between rich and poor is widening, social and economic inequality is growing between regions and between countries. this is because the structural funds tend to decrease. the current community framework has fewer structural funds than the previous one, and in the next framework, everything seems to indicate that the money devoted to cohesion will also be reduced. and this is all the more serious in that the next framework also shows the need to link the application of these funds to structural reforms, to the european semester, and this of course represents the death of the only instrument which allowed the member states to cope with austerity policies.
mr president, commissioner avramopoulos, ladies and gentlemen, the importance of a global pact on refugees is revealed in three simple facts: first, we have the biggest refugee crisis since world war ii; second, 50 percent of the world's displaced people are on the african continent; third, there are about eight million refugees in just five countries. let 's be clear: we have an unprecedented crisis whose effects are felt above all in the developing countries and for which the international community was not and is not prepared! we must be at the forefront of defending a more coherent and fair system, based on more solidarity and shared responsibility, which protects the most vulnerable, such as children. this has been very well emphasized by my colleague corazza bildt, among others. mr president, this global pact, proposed within the framework of the united nations, can correct the mistakes of the past, can improve the present of millions and anticipate a future in which those who flee hunger and persecution are not left to their own devices but are supported by the world. and the european union cannot shirk its leading role.
mr president, the draft un pact to manage migration and refugee movements makes sense. the question is whether the european union will really make a difference, whether it will open up legal and safe, controlled pathways through the revision of the dublin system, so that immigrants and refugees no longer have to put their lives in danger at the hands of smuggling mafias to reach a safe country. because european governments have actually helped to feed these mafias, not opening up legal and safe channels. it is essential that the european union does not continue to outsource responsibilities as it has done with turkey and libya and that it is indeed judicious in the use of taxpayers' money, particularly in development policies that serve regimes, governments that actually want to give their peoples conditions for development, democracy and progress, rather than repressing them and thus making them refugees and migrants. salih mahmoud osman, the 2007 sakharov prize winner, before he was arrested again in his country, sudan, came here to warn us precisely against these so-called development policies, which rely on criminals like omar al-bashir, as if he were our friend and cooperator with the serious policy in this field.
mr president, mrs mogherini, since 2014 15 907 migrants have died in the mediterranean as of yesterday. those who manage to overcome the walls of fortress europe and reach the territory of the eu will face increasing restrictions on the right of asylum and integration, persecution, violence, discrimination, the shameful and inhumane conditions of detention centres, today's real concentration camps, forced deportation, brutal labour exploitation and, worse, sexual exploitation. those who cannot do so are exploited in the camps of turkey or are fed slave labour in the camps of libya, all financed by the european union. these are the consequences of the criminal, xenophobic and racist immigration policies that the eu imposes and promotes, in violation of international law, human rights, human dignity. the absence of eu contributions to the construction of the compacts will be the biggest contribution to the fact that they guarantee the rights and protection of migrants.
mr president, i think we want and we have to say in all our language that the attack by the united states, the united kingdom and france does not contribute to peace in syria or to a political solution in any way. and the european union cannot whitewash this attack, or else it will be made even more ridiculous than it already is. three leaders, weakened in their countries, decided to follow up this attack as if it were their main purpose. they consulted no one, not even their respective parliaments. they have not informed anyone and have made a ridiculous image of the united nations and its secretary-general, the same ridiculous image they have made of the european union, the european commission, the european parliament, which was never consulted, and, curiously, what the commission and parliament are doing is saying that we must speak with one voice. there were two european leaders who spoke alone, yes, they spoke with one voice, but they did not consult, they did not inform the european union at any time. the tragedy of syria is that nobody cares about the syrian people, that is the biggest tragedy and we cannot agree to a military escalation and we cannot whitewash this attack.
mr president, the use of chemical weapons cannot be tolerated anywhere. but as we've seen before, indiscriminate bombing of assad's arsenal, as surgical as it is, makes no strategic difference in ending the war in syria, or punishing the criminals, or preventing further chemical attacks. we need the european union to act with political determination, strategic autonomy and military muscle to enforce the prohibition of chemical weapons and, above all, to exercise its responsibility to protect the syrian people, the people who remain in syria. before we pay for the reconstruction in syria and encourage the return of refugees, we need a political solution to the conflict, yes. but make no mistake, it will not be found, and we will not help find it, if we do not stop the russians, the iranians, the saudis, the turks, the americans, the israelis, assad and terrorist groups from daesh to al-qaeda, from continuing to use syrian soil for crusades at the expense of the syrian people. the european union member states have forces they can mobilise within the framework of the europe of security and defence to impose no-fly zones in syria, to open and defend humanitarian corridors and to bring assad and other criminals in syria to the international criminal court. that is what mrs mogherini should propose to the member states. this is what they must be confronted with. no more tolerance of impunity, no more lack of europe.
mr president, the syrian tragedy is probably the most striking expression of the chaotic situation affecting the international system today. as someone here has already said, it is, in fact, the greatest failure of the international community in recent years. eight years of a terrible war, with no prospect of a solution in sight. this military intervention is justified in view of the chemical weapons attack perpetrated by the syrian government against its own people. but in fact it has not changed anything in the military situation or the political situation in syria. and those can only be changed by a grand diplomatic solution. the european union has that responsibility to lead that diplomatic process. in this respect, the brussels conference can and must play an absolutely decisive role. as someone has already said, europe is also missing in this process. europe must play a more active role, it must reveal a strategy, it must make a new commitment to the political and diplomatic settlement of the conflict, because there is no other way of resolving it than through politics and diplomacy.
in writing. we condemn in the strongest terms the attacks on syria. these attacks violate international law. in this field they confirm the recurrence of the united states and the major powers of the european union in using false pretexts to justify their warlike adventures which aim no further than imperial domination over the middle east and its oil reserves. this barbaric aggression is in response to the alleged use of chemical weapons by the syrian government. there is currently no evidence of this or any of the other alleged attacks, despite international investigations conducted and approved by the syrian government. it is symptomatic, moreover, that the missiles were launched on the day when the mission of inspectors was to begin its work. while we condemn further this escalation in the conflict, we reaffirm our solidarity with the syrian people who are fighting against the western-funded terrorist forces that have sought since 2011 to repeat the scenario experienced in libya or iraq. we are convinced that peace will speak louder and that the people will soon be able to regain their sovereignty.
madam president, this debate should be about the european union against putin's russia which kills russian journalists like maksim borodin, which has invaded and occupied the territories of ukraine and georgia, which is preventing the security council from acting in syria, which is funding the racist far right in europe and the united states, which is spreading fake news and cyber attacks and threatening invincible nuclear weapons. we need to keep talking to russia, yes. but, of course, with strategic sense, armed with a european magnitsky act, with sanctions directed at those responsible for the murders of sergei, anna, boris, maksim and so many others and for the failed murders of the skripals in salisbury. expelling russian diplomats is not politics. putin retaliated and nothing changed. how long are we going to let our governments increase europe's dependence on russia through projects like nord stream 2? and how long are we going to let eu member states, starting with the uk, continue to be laundries for putin and his oligarchs to invest in europe, to capture governments and institutions and to attack our democracies from within?
mr president, the apparent detente in relations between the two koreas and the prospect of a summit between the north korean leader and the american president in may are welcome, but we must do so with great caution, as has already been said here. there is a peace problem here, but there is also a human rights problem here. the problem of peace has to do with the relationship between the two koreas and has to do with the need for the denuclearisation of north korea in due course and with the resolution of this old problem, which calls into question a democratic society such as south korean society. and then there's the serious human rights problem in north korea. north korea is one of the most tyrannical and closed-minded regimes in the present historical circumstances and therefore all the pressure must continue to be exerted on north korea so that this terrible political regime may give signs of openness towards a development, in the long term, towards a completely different situation from that which is still occurring today. let us be optimistic, but above all let us be very cautious about how this situation develops.
mr president, on 1 march, more than a month ago, two greek soldiers accidentally crossed the border with neighbouring turkey. they were immediately arrested by the turkish authorities and remain in that situation to this day. in other circumstances, situations of this nature have been quickly resolved by dialogue between the two countries. greece and the european union have already appealed to the turkish authorities to release the greek soldiers and return them to their country. turkey has responded negatively to this. in the face of such a scandalous situation, this european parliament cannot but express its profound solidarity with these two greek citizens, and as such european citizens, and urge the turkish authorities to release them immediately. the current turkish regime has been moving away from a democratic model. in recent years we have seen a real process of subversion of turkish democracy carried out by the turkish authorities themselves. the european union must firmly condemn this drift of turkey, but above all, in this particular case, we must express without any hesitation our solidarity with the greek people and with these two greek soldiers in particular.

on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, we are one year ahead of the next european elections, and we feel this is the right moment to address the president of the european council, as someone presenting a leader’s agenda, and the president of the commission, as someone presenting a road map for the european union, in the following terms: we need, once and for all, to have a full match between our vision, our ambition and the means we deliver to meet this ambition. this is not happening yet and this european council was, once again, clear evidence of this. we should ask whether it is possible for europe to become a leading continent in energy transition, in making the best of the digital revolution with full respect of privacy, and turning these into more and better jobs. yes it is possible for europe to be the leading continent, but is this being delivered to all regions and cities of europe? no, this is not the case. then we should ask whether there is a lack of resources to meet this ambition. no, there is not a lack of resources: we need to go to the new sources of taxation. all the discussion on digital tax makes a lot of sense, and we do not understand why we are still hesitating about that. we need to move forward, because the sources are there. the first test will be the level of ambition to be set for the next multiannual financial framework, the community budget, because of course we need to address the new needs of external action. stronger means, including defence. but this should not be done by sacrificing the commitment with cohesion policy, which must remain strong in europe. but let me tell you that the bigger test will be about the eurozone, because once and for all – this is the heart of the problem in europe now – we cannot have a eurozone where the possibilities to invest in the future are completely different. this is creating great inequality, so we need to provide not only a completed banking union – yes, we need to complete that – but we need to integrate the european stability mechanism into the community framework. this is the moment to move in this direction. we need to provide the fiscal capacity to enable all members of the eurozone to invest in the future and create better jobs. if you do not address this problem, the ground is there for populist nationalist forces to go on growing all over europe. so we need to go to the heart of the problem, to overcome the eurozone crisis once and for all, and to enable europe to bring real european solutions for european citizens. we believe that now is the time to move – before the european elections.
on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, the scandal of facebook and cambridge analytica is a wake—up call. all of a sudden, everybody understands that the digital revolution involves opportunities but also risks, and sometimes threats, of creating companies without corporate responsibility, of creating jobs without proper social protection and now about taking on board our personal data and using these for the purpose of economic and political manipulation. we just cannot accept this. we need to regain control of our lives and europe must take the lead in this. this is the right moment to invent our european way for the digital revolution and to regain digital sovereignty. and the only way to regain digital sovereignty is through full use of the european scale. we have taken the first step. the s&d group played a pioneering role, calling in this parliament for the regulation on general data protection, but of course this is just the first step. we know now there is an important regulation in the hands of the council on e—privacy, and we urge the council to start a trilogue immediately, because the urgency is clear. of course we want mr zuckerberg to appear in front of us in the european parliament to give proper explanations and proper guarantees, but i believe – and also have expectations regarding the commission– that we need a broader approach in order to pave the way for our european agenda on digital governance, starting with a proper assessment of how social networks work, and need to encourage european innovation in this area. this has to do with how we deal with artificial intelligence. most of all, we would make sure that all citizens can count on generalised digital skills to master the major developments which are taking place.
mr president, this is not a debate about social networks, it is a debate about freedom and democracy. over the last century, we've built a society, a culture and an economy based on information, but we haven't thought about an ethic to protect data. in fact, most of this house actually prevented real privacy protection in the digital age, and governments are now blocking it. i'm not sure. algorithms and machine learning software are now a weapon in political combat. the internet is full of algorithms that analyze and predict our behavior through obscure software. we're not just talking about spying on us or stealing our data, we're talking about software that uses our data to determine what articles we have access to, who we keep in touch with, who can keep in touch with us. in short, this has a name, and that name is manipulation. cambridge analytica has clearly shown us that this is a multi-billion dollar business, and if we do nothing else, those with the money will continue to have access to power.
mr president, big data may be the oil of the 21st century, but there is a fundamental difference between the black gold of the last century and the information we share online today. when we talk about data protection and privacy, we are not talking about a resource, but about a fundamental right of european citizens. a right protected by the charter of fundamental rights and by the constitutions of several member states. the criminal access of cambrige analytica to private information of facebook users is an attack on the fundamental right to data protection of european citizens who have had their privacy violated. if it is serious in absolute terms in each of the almost three million cases in europe, it is even more serious when we look at the potential consequences. when we know that the data of one million and one hundred thousand britons was breached and used to design the brexit campaign, in which leave won by little more than that, we cannot help but draw some worrying conclusions. if that is the case, and if the most important decision in the last decades on european integration was influenced by a crime, then we are facing an attack on the rule of law. that is why facebook owes an explanation to this parliament. that is why the commission cannot fail to act. and that is why citizens cannot ignore their rights, which will be strengthened with the entry into force of the general data protection regulation. (the speaker accepts the answer to a question on the 'blue card' (rule 162 (8)).
answer according to the "blue card" procedure. thank you very much for your question. let's be clear, we can't go back to the last century and it's clear that information is power and information with rules can be business. i'm not against it. what i'm against is the use of personal data without people's consent. each of us owns our data, and that's what's important to safeguard. we have european legislation coming into force, we have to ensure that all those who intervene on the european market comply with that legislation.
mr president, the misuse of facebook data calls for a strong response at the level of rules and at the level of ethics. the example of data protection shows that the european union wins when it anticipates. when we are pioneers we can lead and this is an opportunity to reclaim a relevant voice in the digital globalization, with a strong technological commitment and with a differentiation by values, affirming our digital identity, own, ethical, fair and trustworthy. differentiation by values and translation into legal and technological solutions is the way i propose to respond to the challenge we face. the protection of citizens' rights and democratic transparency are part of our matrix of shared values. we must therefore require platforms operating in the european market to embrace in their models and algorithms the shared values that shape the european digital identity. the debate around data has alerted more european citizens to the issue. we need convincing technological and policy responses. it is time to value europe's digital identity.
madam president, facebook is not just an american company. it operates on a world market, many thousands of users, if not millions, are european, it is truly a global company. to that extent, mark zuckerberg owes an explanation not only to the us congress. you owe the european parliament an explanation. to say that only anyone who wants to join facebook is at the very least ridiculous, when it's not, the worst of arguments because facebook really has no competition, it gives no alternative. it would be like saying that only those who want electricity have it at home. obviously people need electricity and global communications are now an inevitability and this scandal is of a gravity that cannot be resolved with a simple apology. the use and manipulation of personal data for political purposes without the informed consent of the data subject is unacceptable in a democratic state governed by the rule of law. to that extent, the confession of using the data of 87 million facebook users, but not just facebook, to influence campaigns around the world, simply cannot happen. it cannot happen in the united states of america, it certainly cannot happen in europe. and we, who are politicians, and we who are going to vote in 2019, are the first to ensure that this will not happen again.
madam president, this scandal is awakening citizens to the tremendous risks and opportunities of the digital dependence of the economy and of the whole of our current way of life, facebook, big data, the internet of things, artificial intelligence, fundamental rights and genuine elections, infrastructure security, etc. are at stake. this time europe is leading with the gdpr, which is about to come into force and has a global reach. the e-privacy regulation is late in the council. facebook, cambridge analytica, palantir and other companies are making money off of our privacy, and they're lending their algorithms to more sinister feats of social and political manipulation and discrimination. they have chinese and russian competitors that they don't care about today for the nightmare of tomorrow. breaking monopolies and preventing and heavily sanctioning abuses against citizens and democracy, making digital platforms and targeted advertising, paying taxes is more than a european design: it is important to save democratic control and humanity in the globalized era. the commission must act, and now.
in the three years since the adoption of the fourth anti-money laundering and terrorist financing directive, we have revised it and adopted the fifth directive, seeking to deal with the terrorist threat and the revelations of the panama papers. the fifth directive represents a strengthening of control and transparency mechanisms, largely thanks to the persistence of the european parliament, which managed, over eight months of negotiations, to persuade the majority of member states to accept a large part of its requirements. it was parliament which ensured public access to the registers of beneficial owners of companies in the european union, with the aim of ending the opacity of shell companies, used to evade tax and launder money. we have lifted the veil on trusts, even though public access to their beneficiaries is restricted to those who have a legitimate interest, as required by the council. we are guaranteeing more scrutiny and supervision of the professional and service providers, who, as we know, are instrumental in tax evasion and money laundering schemes, and should not be left to self-regulation, which regulates nothing. we have included more market operators among those with due diligence duties towards their clients, such as art dealers and all those who provide tax advice, we have restricted the use of prepaid cards, we have created better cooperation mechanisms between authorities to facilitate transnational investigations and we have created a list of public functions that designate who are the politically exposed persons in the european union subject to enhanced due diligence. all applicants for gold visas and similar schemes in the member states will have to be subject to enhanced scrutiny. but it is also important to stress what we have not achieved, because of the refusal of the member states, such as the creation of a european financial information unit and the inclusion in the central registers of beneficiaries of companies not established in the european union but which conduct significant business here. we have missed an outcome which could have a global impact on financial transparency under the responsibility of the council. too often in these negotiations we have the feeling that some of our governments do not really want to combat money laundering, but rather to facilitate it, captured by interests. the adoption of this directive is indeed a step in the right direction, but much remains to be done. and let's not kid ourselves, many of the new member states are captured by sinister interests, like the mafia that controls e-gambling and invests in cryptocurrencies.
mr president, despite the tax scandals which have revealed to the world the ecosystem which promotes opacity and anonymity and which allows money laundering, the european union remains conditioned by the interests of large multinational companies. the basic measures recommended by the majority of those working to combat money laundering and the financing of terrorism are systematically thwarted by those who want to change just a few things so that everything remains the same. the proposals naturally and always fall far short of expectations and what is needed. i simply ask: how long will we tolerate economic and financial interests being put above the rights of citizens and the people? that's the question i leave to the audience.
mr president, this regulation has of course positive aspects, but it commits an original sin, which is to focus all its attention on the product, while omitting the social and environmental component associated with organic production. in other words, this regulation in practice promotes the production of organic products on an industrial scale, which are also export-oriented, thus seriously undermining a fundamental principle which should link organic production to a production model based on small and medium-sized farms and short-term production and marketing. we have of course given this regulation the benefit of the doubt, and the future will tell whether our doubts were justified or not.
mr president, commissioner, it is essential that organic certification of foodstuffs reflects a production method that meets consumer expectations. the text we are voting on tomorrow is the result of lengthy negotiations and reflects the balance that can be struck in this respect. i welcome the inclusion of cork and sea salt stoppers in the list of products eligible for organic certification. i do, however, have one caveat. the european union covers the outermost regions, whose production conditions are similar to those of certain third countries. often there are imports from these third countries that are allowed to use the organic logo, but this is not possible for the same products from european territory. i admit that this is a complex issue, but i understand that it has not been fully resolved in this dossier, to the detriment of the competitiveness of some european producers.
the revision of this regulation, mr president, brings some development to the sector in terms of modernising and harmonising organic farming practices at european level. the acceptance of sea salt and natural cork stoppers is good news for portugal, but there is much to be done. although european citizens are increasingly demanding safe food, we cannot always conclude that only organic food is healthy, because recent studies have found more chemical residues in organic food than in conventional food and this must be avoided. the controls by the competent authorities must be increased and i have some reservations about the transition periods to organic farming, which are too long in the case of greenhouses, and the fact that a common european limit value for the presence of unauthorised substances has not been set, which could always be an obstacle to the free movement of organic products within the union. i would like to conclude, commissioner, by advocating the strengthening of training measures on organic farming and by stressing the enormous importance of this new regulation for agricultural production in the outermost regions, which, as we know, is already sustainable and organic. now we'll have to work on your certification.
member of the commission. – mr president, i want to thank all of the members who participated in this evening’s debate, and have indeed participated in many debates on this issue over the years. i also want to take the opportunity to thank the estonian and maltese presidencies, as others have done, for their great work. my sense, having listened to the debate, is that there is broad support for the compromise agreement that has been reached by the three institutions. as mr andrieu and mr lins noted, this compromise is the fruit of four years of work and eighteen trilogues. so it is inevitable that such a compromise doesn’t satisfy everybody because you will have views stretching from mr agnew’s views to mr de castro’s views and other views as well which have to be satisfied as much as possible. however, this outcome is, as i said earlier, a reasonable one. otherwise, we would have thrown away all the good work and all of the progress we’ve made over those four years, and i don’t think that would be a responsible way to act. this brings about much needed improvement in the regulatory environment and framework for organic production in europe. it shouldn’t be forgotten that the reason why this commission proposal was originally brought forward by my predecessor mr ciolos was because there was a scandal associated with the imports of organic materials into some of our member states in the european union which was concealed for a long time. this imported organic produce had the potential to damage the organic sector in europe. this was the backdrop to more ambitious proposal originally brought forward by the commission. whatever one’s view about the desired level of ambition, there can be little or no doubt that the text now on the table represents a lot of progress. i reject therefore, mr zullo’s criticisms. in particular, i reject any suggestion that the commission yielded to any interest. you can point the finger somewhere else; we didn’t yield to any interest, whether from northern europe or from anywhere else. what the commission did was to engage in good faith in this democratic process with the members of this house and the council, and the outcome on which you will vote tomorrow is the result of that progress. the potential of the organic sector has yet to be fully realised. this legislation can help, as mr jakovčić said, by providing harmonisation, simplification and standardisation, which is contrary to the claim made by ms noichl. as the sector grows, i hope that the european union can become an exporter of organic products, which need not, as mr viegas suggested, be a threat to the sector. your rapporteur and his colleagues have done a good job in bringing about a lot of improvements in the sector and we have a balanced outcome i feel that we can deliver for our organic sectors in the future, and our organic farmers in particular. we have a legal text which can only help the organic sector. i commend this text to the house and i hope it will get a strong vote here in this house when it is voted upon tomorrow.
the organic production method is based on the principle of making agricultural production compatible with the promotion of biodiversity and the preservation of ecosystems. in addition, organic farming has other virtues, such as protecting the health of consumers and promoting a more sustainable agricultural model, because it is based on small and medium-sized farming and family farming, as opposed to the agribusiness model based on intensive production and the high incorporation of synthetic chemical additives. the new regulation, which contains positive elements, whether in the extension of the production spectrum or in the clarification of certain concepts, points, however, in a direction which in some way subverts characteristics of organic farming, in particular by paving the way for a certain industrialization of the organic production method. taking advantage of the positive impact of a label, resulting from a positive perception of organic products for growing layers of consumers, and the associated economic gains, it is embarking on a path that distorts the concept of organic farming as we understand it today. the focus is solely on the product, ignoring the social and environmental component that has always been associated with organic farming. instead of promoting local agriculture and short-term production and marketing, the aim is to fit organic farming into an export-oriented model which we are not following.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would begin by saying that it is already, i would say, a tradition in this parliament to report on the relationship of parliament with national parliaments and in particular on the application of the treaty of lisbon. this is the fourth or fifth report we have on this subject. the first point i would like to make is that it is clear today, even at the level of cosac, and this is reflected in the report, it is clear today that there is a much better relationship between the national parliaments and the european parliament, that both levels of institutions have understood that they are working together to give democratic legitimacy to the european union. what is the first news in this report? it is indeed a question of interpreting the role of national parliaments in a constitutional sense, i would say. until now we have known that national parliaments play a role at european level, but we have not understood why, what the reason was, and here it is very clearly stated that national parliaments have a different vocation from that of governments: the governments are represented in the council of ministers and the european council, but national parliaments have the advantage of reflecting internal diversity. i am not saying that the european parliament should be the only institution in the world to have a parliamentary representation, but that it should be the only institution in the world to have a parliamentary representation. that is why two proposals are made in this report. the first is that representation in interparliamentary meetings should always be proportional, that is to say, there should be representatives of the majority in parliament but also of the minority, and the second is that in the opinions which parliaments give, and particularly when they want, for example, to activate the yellow card on initiatives relating to the commission, there should be the will of the majority in parliament, but there should be a dissenting vote, there should be a defeated vote by the minorities. that is to say, to bring the political arena that exists at home to the european level. that is the specific role of national parliaments. then there is another important development on the question of the early warning system and the yellow card, because all the national parliaments have told us that they have finally had reservations about the eight-week deadline. we cannot change the treaty, but we can accommodate these eight weeks, and that is why we are calling on the european commission to recognise that the eight-week period should not be counted in the holiday period and, moreover, that a technical period should be allowed so that all parliaments receive translated versions of legislative initiatives at the same time. only then can we activate the yellow card and finally give it the status it has. finally, i would like to say on this point that there are several amendments here, in particular from the socialist group and the liberal group, which must be rejected because they say: 'well, national parliaments do not use the eight weeks because they do not want to'. this report should send a positive signal to the national parliaments, and if they say that eight weeks is not enough, we can extend that period, even if only a little, we must do so, and therefore reject these amendments by the socialist and liberal groups, which are amendments against the national parliaments. then, finally, i would like to say that we are also making a proposal here, not to allow the green card, but to allow suggestions from national parliaments to the european commission on legislative initiatives, that we are advocating here a great strengthening of ipex, in the sense that all national parliaments have updated and detailed information on european matters and, finally, i think that is one of the most constructive proposals, is the idea of having a european week, that is, a week simultaneously in the 27 national parliaments which is a week to celebrate european issues. the presidents, the commissioners, the vice-presidents, the members of the european parliament of different nationalities in the 27 capitals, in the same week, at the same time, throughout europe, are organising a european week, in which there can be, let us say, this reflection on these issues. it would be a way of making mr president, the treaty of lisbon not only exacerbated the inequality in the power relations between the states, strengthening the larger ones, but it also constituted a significant attack on the power of the national parliaments, since powers which were theirs were usurped for the sphere of the european union. in recent years, with the budgetary treaty, the european semester and the economic governance package, this usurpation has been taken even further, for example in the budgetary field, but not only, in that it constitutes a disturbing attack on democracy even at the merely formal level. the role which the treaty of lisbon is alleged to have given to national parliaments, that of mere monitoring of the principle of subsidiarity, is largely a sham, and its futility is quite clear. even where parliaments raise objections, the european commission ignores these objections and maintains its proposals. it is therefore necessary to dismantle this farce and not to insist on it, and to return to the national parliaments the power that has been taken away from them.
rapporteur. – madam president, it is an honour to be shared first with our colleague from the committee on constitutional affairs, mr castaldo, and for the vice-president responsible for national parliaments. so i think this is a fortunate occasion. thank you very much. senhora presidente, eu gostaria só de sublinhar dois aspetos que aqui foram aflorados no debate antes de, naturalmente, agradecer o contributo de todos e, em particular, também as palavras do comissário hogan mas queria sublinhar o seguinte: o primeiro ponto, que me parece muito importante, é o de chamar a atenção para que é muito importante que a comissão, no exame que faz da subsidiariedade, seja um pouco mais aberta. na verdade, a ligação entre subsidiariedade e proporcionalidade é extremamente difícil de fazer e, por isso, deveria haver um self-restraint quando se corre logo para negar qualquer cartão amarelo dizendo que está em causa a proporcionalidade e não a subsidiariedade. isto era o aspeto que deveria ser tido em conta. isto não desmente em nada o que diz o colega jáuregui atondo porque a subsidiariedade em muitos casos implica entregar a competência à comissão europeia, à união europeia e não aos estados nacionais e não aos parlamentos nacionais. mas, em muitas situações, invoca-se a proporcionalidade como um pretexto para não fazer uma verdadeira aferição da subsidiariedade. este era um aspeto que eu gostaria de marcar aqui. e o segundo aspeto era insistir na ideia de que dar mais algum tempo e alguma folga aos parlamentos nacionais é justo e é devido, e é justo e é devido por esta razão: nem todos os parlamentos nacionais têm os recursos que têm, por exemplo, o parlamento alemão ou o parlamento sueco e portanto, naturalmente, a forma como em determinadas circunstâncias se apreciam certas matérias também depende de país para país e de estrutura para estrutura e, portanto, criar um pouco mais de tempo permite colocar todos os parlamentos em condições de igualdade e permite um diálogo maior entre os vários parlamentos. há um ponto que gostaria de sublinhar, no qual me revejo e quase todos os colegas os sublinharam, que é: naturalmente que o grande papel dos parlamentos nacionais é no controlo dos seus governos. esta é que é a questão. se eles forem capazes de o fazer com proficiência tenho a certeza de que a união europeia funcionará melhor.
on behalf of the s&d group. madam president, acting together to address vaccine reservations and reverse the decline in vaccination rates in the european union is a priority. i have in fact sent a question to the european commission in this connection, i raised a number of questions in the recent debate in the envi committee, and the resolution we are debating today is in line with what i have been told in all these circumstances. i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, mrs elena gentile, on her excellent work, and i would like to congratulate the commissioner on the extraordinary effort he has put into solving this problem. the conditions are in place so that together, the european parliament, the member states and the european commission can put in place consistent policies that actually tackle the problem. strengthening the legal basis of the european vaccination plan, developing a joint purchasing platform which already has 19 countries and which must be developed, implementing data sharing practices so that there is better coordination and informed coordination, developing research on this subject in an open, transparent way to increase citizens' confidence and also the reliability of vaccines, integrating these measures into a wider plan for health promotion at european level, also taking into account, for example, animal vaccination and the fight against antibiotic resistance, launching a european information and education campaign on this important subject for public health and the quality of life of all europeans, this is what we have the instruments to do today. we must be practical and capable of putting into practice measures which improve the concrete lives of citizens. this is an example, it needs to be done quickly, decisively, transparently, because it is important for people, because it is important for the future of europe and for the health of the world.
madam president, in 2017, measles killed 35 people in a group of 50 countries in the european region where more than 20,000 cases of the disease were recorded. a high incidence of cases is estimated in children under one year of age who are still too young to receive the first dose of the vaccine. hence the importance of herd immunity. this is compromised when a significant portion of the population is not vaccinated, a situation that harms not only those who have chosen not to be vaccinated, but also those who cannot be immunized. vaccination has helped eradicate diphtheria, congenital rubella, and neonatal tetanus. in 2016, portugal was officially recognised as being a book of measles, but is now facing the third outbreak of the disease in two years. there is a strong and systematic need for training and awareness-raising measures by public health services, necessarily coordinated at european level, to combat misinformation and prejudice whose harmful effects are becoming evident and to encourage vaccination.

mr president, mr president of the commission, mr commissioner, i should like to begin by emphasizing the work the commission has done in seeking to bring together, balance, competitiveness and territorial and social cohesion. the budget must have a competitive aspect, but it must never forget internal solidarity. this is a proposal which represents a good basis for work, it is a good starting point for negotiations where unanimity in the council and a favourable opinion from the european parliament are required. it has positive points: the strengthening of erasmus+, the increase also in the budget for interrail, the priority for youth, the strengthening also of research and innovation, economic and monetary union and the possibility of a fund to combat symmetrical to asymmetrical shocks, a programme for reforms, the strengthening of security, support for migration, the simplification that is advocated and also the end of rebates. but there are also points where we feel it is necessary to go further. it is important that cohesion policy is not reduced and that the common agricultural policy is also kept within its current envelope. and i ask the member states if they are prepared to cut their national budgets, if they are prepared to cut the amounts they receive under cohesion policy and under the common agricultural policy, because cohesion policy is also essential for investment in the european union.

mr president, commissioner, our position has been very clear from the outset on the pressure on cohesion policy. there could not and should not be any reduction that would harm cohesion policy. commissioner, i understand the new challenges of a union of 27, and i would like to stress here the focus and priority given to security, border protection, research and support for young people through erasmus+ and interrail. but there are two negatives: when we talk about supporting businesses, small and medium-sized enterprises, the digital economy, we are missing a funding line for tourism that is not mentioned. and i cannot but regret this announced cut in cohesion policy. we are talking about a cut of about 5% in cohesion policy and the cap and it is crucial to keep the axis of the outermost regions in cohesion policy, because we are talking about solidarity with the outermost regions which have increased costs in mobility, education, health, and we need this solidarity from the union which is not in this document.
the european commission presented its draft budget for the period 2021-2027 yesterday. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the establishment of a european agency for the evaluation of medicinal products for human use (emah) is a step in the right direction. if, on the one hand, the commission is trying to respond to the needs arising from new phenomena which directly challenge the european union's capacity to act, whether in the field of economic competitiveness or in the field of the security of citizens, on the other hand, cohesion policy is clearly being sacrificed, with the resulting penalisation of the effort of convergence between the richer and poorer regions. the relegation of the convergence objective to the background could lead to an accentuation of the two major geographical dividing lines affecting europe today: the north-south and west-east lines. we are, however, only at the beginning of a process which is expected to be complex, protracted and highly controversial.
the multiannual financial framework of the european union takes as strategic elements all the guidelines for deepening capitalist integration, which is responsible for unequal, unfair and asymmetrical development between the different countries in the european union and which has accentuated portugal's divergence in this framework. time and life have shown that the european union funds have never compensated (nor could compensate) for the loss of sovereignty, the destruction of national production and the compromise of the country's development possibilities, which resulted and result from the single market, the euro and the common policies of the eu. this is especially true at a time when there are plans to cut funding to countries such as portugal, which have been the most affected by integration, in favour of increasing the number of items which benefit the main beneficiaries of integration, the eu's major powers and their economic groups. the proposal for the 2021-2027 multiannual financial framework presented today by the european commission thus creates the conditions for further accentuating the divergence which has characterised the eu, with the increase in inequalities and asymmetries between member states and within each member state. it also fuels the militaristic escalation and the security drift into which the major european powers are plunging.

i would like to stress the commitment and the work of the rapporteurs, in particular my colleague, mrs ulrike rodust, in this key document for implementing the common fisheries policy, and also echo the words of the previous speakers and the commissioner. i would like to stress the importance of the most up-to-date scientific information and the inclusion of recreational fishing data in the scientific information to be taken into account in the scientific councils of the international council for the exploration of the seas and the scientific, technical and economic council for fisheries of the european commission. it is important that we approve the multiannual plan and put it into action, even though we recognise that it came out of the reduced trilogue of some of the provisions adopted in the european parliament on the date it was adopted. one of the handicaps that i find somewhat problematic is that the political agreement reached has reduced the ability to keep fishing limits below fmsy reference points. another is the lack of emphasis on the recovery of source species, thus concentrating on target species only. but you have to balance the great with the viable, with the negotiable. after several months of negotiations, to reverse a plan which, while not perfect, is nevertheless a very, very important advance, would be a mistake. in this context, i consider it absolutely essential that the multiannual plan should continue at this stage. it seems to me that the possible and important basis has been laid for the plan of specific measures to be presented by the regional organisations, taking into account not only the sustainability of stocks but also the protection of sensitive species and, of course, the income of fishing communities with interests in this area of fishing.
mr president, multiannual plans are instruments for fisheries management with indisputable virtues, capable of providing some temporary stability to fishing activity, which is characterized by instability, something which the mere adoption of the quota system alone does not guarantee, but the approval of this multiannual plan makes it inevitable that once again the limits imposed by an institutional framework which, wrongly, defines the management of living marine resources as an exclusive competence of the european union will be recognized. this has largely determined the persistence of a centralised fisheries management system which is inadequate to the interests of local fisheries, particularly small-scale fisheries, and which favours industrial fishing, together with a definition of management objectives which is not always soundly supported from a scientific point of view. the declared aims of regionalizing or even decentralizing the common fisheries policy have come to nothing, a reality that is inseparable from the deepening economic and social suffocation of fisheries and coastal communities.
i would also like to congratulate the two rapporteurs on these two reports. we are therefore discussing in this debate two documents on fishery products, the value chain, control and access to markets. all of these aspects are particularly relevant in the context of the outermost regions, such as the azores, where i come from. as such, the ops are widely considered in the articulation of both reports of this parliament. i would like to stress that it is crucial that the transport of fish from the ors to the national and international markets is properly supported in order to ensure fair competition with other fish products, whether fresh or processed. this is the only way to achieve convergence and fairer access to markets and to maintain cohesion in the value chain. i would again stress the possibility of creating a financial instrument that would provide specific support for the fisheries sector, for example by restoring the posei fisheries programme, with the capacity to effectively improve the potential of the outermost regions for sustainable commercial fishing.
the fisheries sector is of strategic importance for the public fish supply, for the balance of the food balance in several member states and for the development and well-being of coastal communities. the uncertainty of the income of fishermen, due to the way marketing is carried out in the sector, the way prices are formed at first sale and the irregular characteristics of the activity, implies, among other aspects, the need to maintain adequate public funding for the national and community sector. mechanisms are needed to improve the first selling price, so as to benefit fishermen by increasing the remuneration of their work and to promote a fair and adequate distribution of value added through the value chain of the sector, reducing intermediation margins, enhancing the prices paid to production and constraining the prices paid to final consumption. it is necessary to improve the stability of markets and to support the transformation and development of new products and marketing methods. these are the proposals and contributions which the report welcomes and which we would therefore like to highlight and value here.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, this report opens a new round of debate and negotiation on the cap. we need a fairer policy, one which responds to challenges such as climate change, but above all we need an agricultural policy which persists in its common strand and which effectively meets the needs of farmers, because there is no agriculture and no rural world without farmers. member states should continue to have a margin of manoeuvre in the first pillar of the cap to grant support linked to sectors of particular social, economic and environmental relevance. it is essential to continue adjusting national direct payment allocations towards greater convergence between member states. i call for a robust european budget for european agriculture to be maintained, with no national co-financing in the first pillar of the cap. finally, i would like to see the special situation of the outermost regions taken into account in the process of reforming the cap.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, this report is important and timely. we must remember that in europe there is one farmer under 35 for every nine over 55. in some countries, such as portugal, spain, italy, the united kingdom and bulgaria, the proportion of young farmers is very low, only one farmer in 20 over 55. the problem is obviously the maintenance of our food self-sufficiency, and the problems are mainly the establishment of farmers, access to land, the transfer of holdings from the oldest to the youngest and access to credit. finally, as amendments we have tabled, there are a number of suggestions which make sense to us, such as the creation of a programme similar to erasmus, combined with vocational training to improve skills and the exchange of experience among young farmers, support for young people who want innovative production techniques and processes. finally, no one questions the strategic nature of agriculture, but there is a serious abandonment of land and young people in rural areas must be given the same conditions as young people in urban areas. i would like to ask, in conclusion, whether it is understandable that, for example, in my country, farmers receive less money for the sale of their products today than in 1986, when portugal joined the european union. certainly, when that is the case, farming is not attractive.
mr president, generational renewal and knowledge transfer are fundamental to agriculture, because in this way we can bring in new people, new practices, new technologies and better ways of producing agricultural products at lower costs, with less risk to the environment, to human health and in a sustainable way. however, this renewal does not mean taking away from one to put on to another, but rather to integrate the new knowledge into the age-old practices of our most experienced farmers who have contributed a great deal to the public good of agriculture. we must not forget that in many cases young people are already working on family farms but unfortunately they are rarely able to have their own farm because of the money involved in setting up new farmers. i therefore believe that young farmers should receive increased support whenever they take over the responsibility for family farms, so that they can continue to do so, especially since young people have greater difficulties in accessing capital. it is therefore essential that the european union should allow a dignified withdrawal of the more experienced sector with clear support, thus making room for the younger ones.
mr president, commissioner, agriculture in europe, like any other economic activity, needs young people, as has been said here, who can provide the necessary generational renewal to keep the sector going with greater dynamism and openness to innovative solutions. in addition to the socioeconomic trends that may account for the ageing of europe's fields, access to land, vocational vocation, access to insurance and credit are factors that determine the integration of young people into agriculture. it is essential to provide member states with appropriate instruments under the cap to promote the rejuvenation of the agricultural sector through specific measures under the first and second pillars. it is also important, and this should be emphasized, that national policies can reinforce these mechanisms with policies facilitating access to credit, leasing, land inheritance and tax incentives with the aim of attracting young people and re-skilling the rural fabric.
mr president, the commission is pursuing its strategy of gilding the pill of the policies it proposes. adjectives are a bit overkill, but practice doesn't go with your speech. instruments such as the 'connecting europe' mechanism or the so-called trans-european transport network have served as a drain on public money for the private sector or have fuelled the disastrous public-private partnerships, political strategies that have promoted the privatisation of public service provision, the disinvestment in equipment and infrastructure, the degradation and the increase in the cost of the service provided to the population, the precariousness of labour relations. the commissioner spoke of a financial package which would allow decisive responses to be made to the development of the sector. these are false promises when we know the budgetary machete that the commission is preparing for the next mff, particularly in relation to cohesion, which we have known for a long time has never been the real objective of the european union. the increase in funds for cohesion free of conditionalities is required, allowing countries like portugal strong public investments in the sector, policies and public responses that serve the needs of the populations and the development of the country.
mr president, in the interinstitutional agreement on better law-making, competitiveness is the first and foremost criterion for determining the appropriateness and content of european union legislation. nothing new, just the assumption of a long-held principle, now stripped of any social or environmental rhetoric. it is not the interests of smes that are being defended, but those of big capital, of the big economic groups, of the major european powers. as expected, impact assessments have been and are subverted and undervalued. the democratic nature of the legislative and decision-making process is being further limited, which has opened the door to a greater concentration of power in the european commission and to the limitation of the capacity of legislators, especially members of parliament, to intervene. the perverse amendments to the rules of procedure of the european parliament limit the ability of members to speak. the transparency of the legislative process has also been duly reduced. it is in the shadows that the powers that be assert themselves and it is for them that this agreement is needed, it is for them that we want to legislate better.
on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, the european institutions cannot adopt a budget for the future which we will cut in cohesion and convergence. this is just unacceptable, and we need to take into account the situation we have. of course we need to address the challenges, but we need absolutely to keep a budget which reduces social inequalities and keeps the opportunity to invest in the future. this should happen for the european union as a whole, but also for the eurozone. and let me make a special call on the european commission, which is adopting in two days, finally, something to equip the eurozone with fiscal capacity. you need to be bold, because the eurozone has been suffering with deep divergences for many years. look to the situation we now have in italy. we are on the brink of a disaster – an economic, social, political disaster. some call for leaving the eurozone in order to leave austerity. they are wrong. others are calling to accept to stay in the eurozone, as it deals with austerity. they are also wrong. the solution we need to have is to reform the eurozone to make it a space where people can invest in the future and have again confidence in the future, and we social democrats have a full plan for this, and will be criticising all those who are opposing this necessary reform. so the commission has this opportunity. we need to have a fiscal capacity able to support investment in the future, to support upward economic and social convergence and to protect people from shocks. this is the moment to send this message, because we cannot play with fire.

mr president, commissioner, the european parliament wants a europe that is cohesive in terms of its territory and its social fabric, a united europe, a competitive, secure and united european union. and that word has to be translated into concrete action. we cannot say that cohesion policy is working well and has increased gross domestic product in a number of countries, and even helps all the other member states, and then we will cut this cohesion policy and, more seriously, we will cut those who need it most, we will cut the poorest. i think that the 45% cut in the cohesion fund, which is intended for member states with less than 90% of gross national income per capita, is unacceptable. what is working well must continue, the cohesion policy, the common agricultural policy and, in particular, rural development. a 25% cut is also incomprehensible. no one will be able to explain to voters, for example, that the poorest member states will have higher cuts than the richest member states. we need solidarity to be a de facto solidarity. the added value of the budget is well established: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
this proposal for a multiannual financial framework takes as strategic elements the guidelines which underlie the unequal, unfair and asymmetrical development between the different countries in the european union. the guidelines which, instead of the promised convergence, have only brought divergence. time and history have shown that the european union funds have never compensated (nor could compensate) for the destruction of the productive systems of the periphery, the loss of sovereignty, the asymmetrical impact of the single market, the euro and common policies. nevertheless, what the european commission is now proposing is to cut off the instruments that are supposedly intended to mitigate the divergence. cutting back on cohesion policy. even if it were left as it is, it would not be enough, as you can see. cutting national envelopes in countries with the greatest difficulties. cutting back on agriculture and fisheries. but military spending is increasing. increase funding for the repressive and security apparatus. the appropriations for programmes which benefit the major powers in particular are being increased. everything is tailored to their interests, and it shows the true nature of the european union. this is unacceptable.
in writing. the proposal submitted by the european commission (ec) on 2 may 2018 for the 2021-2027 multiannual financial framework falls far short of the realistic and measured ambition expressed in the resolution adopted by the european parliament on 17 march. the resolution under discussion today is a strong warning from the direct representatives of the european citizens to the institutions. in the political context in which the european union (eu) is living, with positive economic indicators and worrying political signals, it is crucial that the multiannual financial framework reflects a mobilizing and proximity vision capable of fostering convergence, strengthening cohesion and increasing competitiveness on global markets. the lack of consistency in the presentation of some of the priorities and financial allocations in the ec proposal also seriously damages the credibility of the exercise. the commission proposal can be the embryo of a new stage in the consolidation of the european project, but this must be achieved by ensuring the robustness of the approach, the safeguarding of european values and the appropriate financial allocation. it's still possible to get to good port, but we need to correct trajectories and options. i hope that this debate and the resolution that will come out of it will contribute to that.
madam president, the revision of the posting of workers directive, which is being hailed as a major social flag, is, on the contrary, a clear demonstration of how the promotion of labour and social rights is not one of the priorities of the commission, nor of the majority of the european parliament. we reject the negotiated view between the council and parliament which in practice allows for an endless period of secondment to suit the needs of the employer. let us be clear: those who have been working in another country for two years or more are not posted, they are emigrants. the proposal takes responsibility for enforcing labour rights away from companies. the failure to apply explicitly the principle of equal pay for equal work means that the principle of equal pay is not guaranteed, and it opens the door explicitly to the use of temporary work undertakings which, as we know, subsist on the basis of low wages and poor working conditions, that is, they seek to maintain and secure a mobile army of cheap labour from countries such as portugal, which feed the profits of the large european transnational companies.
mr president, in portugal only 2.5% of farmers are under 35 years old, whereas more than 50% of our farmers are over 65. this is the result of the common agricultural policy which has led to the abandonment of thousands and thousands of farmers in portugal, worsening our agri-food deficit. we are naturally in favour of greater support for young farmers, but, let us stop being hypocritical, this debate cannot be dissociated from the cap, because we have a cap which pays for not producing, which has completely liberalised agriculture, opening up world competition and crushing farmers' incomes. so if we want young people in agriculture, then we must create a new cap, based on the defence of small and mediumsized farming and with public instruments for regulating production, which effectively allows young farmers to live off their activity and not on the alms of the european union.

madam president, the catastrophe in libya has no end in sight as long as the european union persists in a policy of deception, financing alleged libyan coastguards, who are nothing more than armed militias, the same ones who traffic and torture migrants and oppress the population. there are no coastguards, no security forces under the control of a non-ruling government or a criminal like haftar. european security and defence and the alleviation of the suffering of the libyan people require that we invest in what we have neglected so far: security sector reform, which implies the demobilization and disarmament of the militias. without this, there will be no governance, no transition strategy that leads to elections in libya, and we will not stop daesh and al-qaeda terrorists or the madkhali salafists increasingly entrenched in libya. it is essential to open up controlled, safe, legal channels of migration so that we do not continue to feed the business of the trafficking mafias. the un, unhcr and aom must step up their intervention in libya, with the support of military forces which must integrate european and african contributions.
the reporter. madam president, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your report. ladies and gentlemen, in 1990, in a small town in luxembourg that would go down in history, the convention implementing the schengen agreement was signed. the president of the european parliament, pieter dankert, asked at the time who 40 years ago could have imagined that france, germany, almost reunited, and the benelux would sign an agreement here in schengen. let me adapt these historical words to the present day. who would have imagined, 20 years ago, that the area of free movement would include 26 countries, 400 million people and 50,000 km of external borders? who would have thought that the european parliament would be debating its report on the state of schengen for the first time today? in 1990, pieter dankert answered your question with a resolution 'we have succeeded'. today, however, we know that schengen is never completed or guaranteed. the full accession of bulgaria and romania remains to be achieved. it is unacceptable that seven years after fulfilling all the conditions for freedom of movement, bulgarians and romanians are still treated by the council as second-class citizens. i hope that tomorrow the european parliament will reaffirm once again that bulgaria and romania should be full members of schengen. ladies and gentlemen, schengen is never really finished or guaranteed, and 33 years after the signing of the schengen agreement we are faced with new dangers and challenges. the last three years have been dramatic. a seemingly new normal has emerged. internal border controls have become part of the daily lives of millions of europeans once again. national governments, along with the most populist fringes of our political spectrum, have made schengen the scapegoat for the failures of security policies and the fragility of the common european asylum system. you put schengen in a coma, carrying evils that aren't his own. to spy on blame and win elections, member states are lightly using the schengen atomic bomb and doing so illegally. the maximum period agreed by the member states themselves has been largely exceeded and the vague and reluctant reasons given are not reflected in reality. if schengen were to disappear, the economic impact could reach eur 50 billion, but above all, the greatest expression of the europe of the citizens would disappear. mr president, the schengen information system is the largest european system for combating crime and controlling our borders. however, some member states continue to fail to comply with the rules on their use. the united kingdom is not alone in this. the commission has already, after the adoption of this report, informed the schengen working party, which i chair, of the serious problems in the use of the sis by the united kingdom. the accession of this state is provisional because it is not a schengen member and is therefore independent of its exit procedure. this is not the first evaluation with serious results. however, it is particularly worrying at the moment because the united kingdom is in the process of leaving but wants to maintain a relationship with the union also in the field of security. here too the council and the commission should work with parliament to find a solution. the commission cannot be afraid to use all the instruments at its disposal and can count on the support of this parliament. with this report we are taking a significant step forward in the exercise of democratic control in schengen, we are increasing transparency and thereby a more serious discussion and, ladies and gentlemen, the problem of combating terrorism is not solved by more borders, it is solved by more cooperation. if national governments do not have the courage to save schengen, let us have the courage to save the freedom of european citizens.
mr president, i thank the rapporteur, mr carlos coelho, and i support everything he has said here, including the use of sis by the united kingdom and the inclusion of romania and bulgaria. in this report we show that the main problems in the functioning of the schengen system are the result of political tacitness, selfishness and lack of solidarity between member states in responding to transnational security challenges, in particular terrorism, which demand more europe and more europe passes through schengen. the european commission, mr avramopoulos, cannot continue to whistle in the wind and ignore schengen violations which undermine the integrity of the system and threaten collective security. from the gold visas in which several member states, including my own country, portugal, are competing viciously, to the case of malta, where there is alarming evidence of organized schemes to sell schengen visas en masse to libyans and algerians, not to mention the shame of the illegal dirty deal made by the european union with turkey. when will the european union comply with schengen and no longer let the trafficking mafias manage the migration flows, as the un secretary-general antónio guterres has denounced? when will the council unblock the revision of the common european asylum system and open legal, controlled, safe channels so that asylum seekers and refugees do not have to put their lives at the mercy of the traffickers that the lack of european management is in fact feeding?
