mr president, i voted in favour of this report because there is an urgent need for balanced and fair regulation of this sector. it seems we're opening a gap between right and wrong. but this directive is not about that at all. it is about recognising the challenges of the digital single market that require the appropriate legislative adaptation and it is about responsible policy. the internet has opened up possibilities in the extent, depth and speed with which we share information, but with it we have been trampling on copyright. digital media can faithfully reproduce audiovisual creations and achieve their infinite dissemination without the proper remuneration of the artists. the copy/paste culture allows for the plagiarism of text and images without copyright references for commercial purposes, which means that someone has been profiting with impunity from the work of others and this has to stop. we may not yet have solved the problem of the sustainability of music and the press in the digital age, but this directive will certainly go a long way towards correcting this injustice. i would like to stress that this directive is not about people's right to information, to culture, and it is not about restricting freedom of expression. this directive is about the fairness required to ensure that creators are properly remunerated and that cultural and information workers are properly recognised.
madam president, the words of laurence fink, ceo of blackrock, when he opened the new year of the german stock exchange in 2017, make it clear what is coming. it said 'workers tend to be too dependent on state pensions'. two years earlier, the same company had recommended to the european union the creation of a crossborder pension fund on its territory. june 2016: the date on which commissioner dombrowskis announced the commission's proposal to create a european personal pension scheme. the report also calls for the commission to take a more active role in the fight against fraud and for the commission to be able to take action to combat fraud. even before any decision on this proposal is taken, blackrock is already making its way into european pension funds by running a pilot project set up by the currency commissioner and handed over to the us multinational. if masks ever had them, they have fallen, leaving the interests of those whom the european commission serves quite exposed. but this is not a debate about blackrock, nor about the commissioners, it is a debate about the future of pensions and the policy options that the european union seeks to impose to the detriment of the people and the workers. the creation of a new pan-european pension product responds to a longstanding claim by large investment funds to appropriate workers' contributions to public social security, integrating the world bank's 'second pillar' growth promotion model. this is a despicable assault on workers' pensions by the european union, which is trying to take over their earnings and liquidate the public and universal social security system with the support of the right and social democracy. the commission's proposal is not surprising, since it is the same group that, in its country-specific recommendations in the context of the european semester, insists on the theorising of the unsustainability of public pension systems and the inevitable replacement of redistributive issues by capitalisation systems. the implementation of these european union recommendations, together with the right-wing and austerity policies followed in recent decades by those who have ruled countries such as portugal, have resulted in an accelerated process of weakening public social security, with obvious and harmful consequences: the attack on the right to social protection, the introduction of measures that erode the basis for financing the systems, particularly harming the elderly, unemployed workers and beneficiaries of non-contributory social benefits. this is a reality aggravated by the policies of low wages and high job insecurity, the destruction of national productive capacity, the increase in unemployment, with inevitable reductions in contributions made and the consequent increase in poverty and social exclusion. the increase in the retirement age, proposed by the european commission, is added. they are inciting a confrontation between generations with one single aim: to privatise the pension systems and to usurp the income of the workers, serving them as a handshake to the vultures of the pension funds, making a clean slate of the brutal social consequences of the bankruptcy of these funds. the needs of workers and people, as opposed to the interests of the large economic and financial groups, require an obvious answer: the defence of a public, universal and solidarity-based social security system as an irreplaceable instrument for promoting social justice in the distribution of income, which must be consolidated by strengthening public systems with contributory financing based on the principles of solidarity between generations and a redistributive logic, a path incompatible with your integration project which cannot be separated from the fight against unemployment and precariousness, the creation of jobs with rights and the effective valorization of wages, the fight against tax evasion and the rejection of the misuse of the revenue from the tax system for other purposes. public and universal social security must serve the interests, well-being and happiness of workers and peoples. its defence and strengthening are fundamental to building a more just and united society in another europe based on progress and social convergence.
on behalf of the s&d group. – madam president, the s&d group welcomes clearly the debate on the pension system in plenary. this is indeed a very important part of the welfare system and this is now the right time for us to design a welfare system for the 21st century. yes, we know that our pension systems are confronted with difficult challenges because of ageing and because the poverty we still have among pensioners is not being reduced. something needs to be addressed because we cannot have poverty involved in the pension system. this is even more acute if we have in mind the future of younger generations because, for the sake of fairness, we need to make sure that young generations have the same chances to count on an adequate pension when they need it. how could we address this? first of all, let me make the point that the best way to have a sustainable pension system is to have a very high employment rate and good quality jobs. then, let me also underline, in the middle of the discussion about the outcomes of migration, that migration, if it is properly managed, can make a positive contribution to the sustainability of the pension system. last but not least, we need to consider new ways to finance the system, notably by corporate taxation and by the so-called digital tax, because a new source of tax is there. let me make the point that the european social model involves a pension system based basically in a public pillar and the pensions system should be available for everyone. this is a key ingredient, even in the digital economy. so this is our main point for the architecture.
madam president, a privileged instrument for the realisation of the ethical-political imperative of solidarity is the existence of a pension system which guarantees dignified conditions in old age. this pension system must be supported first and foremost by the beneficiary himself, through the deductions made throughout his lifetime, but it must also be supported by the contributions and taxes of those who are now at the height of their contributory capacity. this effort should not be seen as a generosity of the present generations, but rather as a payment of a debt of gratitude to our elders. they paid with their taxes and contributions for the schools where we were educated free of charge as children, teenagers and young people, as well as for the health services from which we all benefit. the least we can do today is to make an effort to ensure that they have the economic conditions to end their lives with dignity. on the other hand, a public pension system is not, in itself, better than a private one. it all depends on the effectiveness of the control mechanisms that can be exercised over who manages the money available. it all depends, after all, on the ability to prevent corruption or abuse of power. unfortunately, there are states, even within the european union, which do not set a good example in the management of pension systems.
commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, in recent years we have been redefining the rights to privacy and the protection of personal data in europe. the most visible aspect of this change is the general data protection regulation. with its entry into force this year, we are better protecting european citizens, empowering the union to face the challenges of the future and leading the world in changing to better protect them. cases such as facebook and cambridge analytica make it clear that we were right, but that regulation is only one of the four great pillars of data protection in europe, as has already been mentioned by several speakers. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the data protection rules are applied in the member states. the union and all its bodies must of course set an example, which is why aligning the union's rights and duties with the general data protection regulation was imperative. that's what we do with this new instrument. of course, in very specific cases, such as europol, the data processed need a specific regime, but let us not take the whole thing for granted. this new instrument will undoubtedly protect personal data held by european union bodies, including this parliament, in the same way. the data protection reform in europe will be completed and we will once again reaffirm europe's leadership in the world in defending these fundamental rights. i would like to congratulate mrs ernst on her excellent work.
mr president, the excessive production of waste and waste is inseparable from the current patterns of production and consumption. challenging the excessive production of waste and waste, including single-use plastics, necessarily requires challenging these production and consumption models. it demands that we question, in the final analysis, a system, capitalism, which is based on an irrational and unsustainable relationship between man and nature. the fight against this irrationality does not exclude measures which, although seemingly small, can have a great scope and impact. the only thing that's not free is the big measures. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat pollution, and it is not the commission's intention to take any action to combat it. in this area too, a regulatory approach should be given priority over a market approach, and reuse and recycling, for example in packaging, should be promoted alongside waste reduction. that was the sense of an opinion of the committee on industry for which you were rapporteur. finally, scientific research and technological development also play an important role in the search for more sustainable alternatives to current materials.
mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the plastics obtained from organic polymers were a major breakthrough in chemical engineering in the last century, providing an opportunity for the design of new products because of their malleability, hardness, low cost and, above all, their durability. but if low cost has determined mass production and disposable use, their durability has determined their perpetuation in our seas and oceans. the truth is that, taken together, these characteristics have promoted the environmental disaster that we now recognize and that is so well exposed in this report. the fact is that because of human irresponsibility in the use and disposal of plastic material, we are now experiencing the global drama of ocean pollution with impacts on biodiversity that add to the already difficult consequences of climate change. i would therefore like to congratulate the rapporteur on the excellent work he has done, which will enable us to show with this directive that europe is taking a responsible position to lead the world in this mission of not making an unsustainable situation worse. but that's not enough. and it's not enough because we can't be the only europeans to be concerned about our fishing gear, to use alternatives to single-use plastics, to recycle and to think about eco-design. this issue must be kept on the global agenda of climate diplomacy and more ambitious action in europe's trade relations with third countries must be taken immediately. it is also not enough to have the courage to outline an ambitious strategy and then postpone the concrete measures to pursue it. ladies and gentlemen, what is needed is a change in the behaviour of civil society and the business community must be a partner in this. we need, in this joint effort with the commission and the member states, to make industries part of the solution and to make the modernisation of the production sector more environmentally conscious.
madam president, the debate on the problem of plastics and their impact on the environment is a global problem, but one that the european union has been leading. this fight is not against plastics. what we want to tackle is how they are used and what happens when they are no longer useful. i welcome the commission's proposals to tackle this issue, to which this parliament has been making a decisive contribution. i've been hosting a number of debates on the topic, in particular the impact on the oceans and ways to circumvent it. i agree with the rapporteurs that this issue can only be solved in a comprehensive way and that the circular economy of plastics is an important part of the problem which must be encouraged. to prevent plastics from reaching the sea is to prevent them from reaching our table. so improving the destination of used plastics is one way to reduce that impact.
madam president, commissioner, the definition of one health has expanded the concept of human health to include other factors which influence it, such as animal health and the environment. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's environmental policy is not distorted. the fact is that it has become imperative to stop the rampant use of antibiotics introduced by the intensification of animal husbandry practices. however, while antibiotics are important in ensuring food security, we cannot let options for reducing production costs come at the expense of our health future. as far as human health is concerned, it seems that prevention really is the best medicine. i am convinced that the increased use of vaccines envisaged in the report is all the more timely and i would therefore like to remind you of the number of deaths that could have been prevented in europe this year if it had not been for the anti-science campaign that has discredited vaccines as an essential public health tool. while health systems in the member states face enormous funding challenges, we must continue to invest in rapid diagnostic technologies and research into new antibiotics, especially those that function as last-line therapies to be used only in extreme cases. but in order to have these antibiotics, we're going to have to test new forms of producer compensation that are not based on the volume of drug sales. i'd like to conclude by saying that antimicrobial resistance is a public health issue that civil society needs to be aware of and it's a global issue, and without wanting to create apocalyptic scenarios, today's regulations could prevent a global pandemic of unimaginable impact and scale.
mr president, commissioner, antimicrobial resistance is one of the great challenges of our time and a global threat. it is important to control diseases without compromising the effectiveness of prophylactic means. ultimately, it is important to preserve the capacity to treat disease itself, both in animals and in humans. i would stress that the pollution of water and soil by antibiotics for human and veterinary use is a growing problem for the environment itself and that special attention should be paid to this. i would like to emphasise the importance of member states' commitment to implementing national action plans and creating appropriate incentives to achieve this objective. this effort cannot be dissociated from international cooperation and should be encouraged. it seems to me to be the only possible approach, and it is equally fundamental in terms of research and development.

on behalf of the gue/ngl group, mr president, after a hasty ifrs 4 standard which left out the measurement of liabilities, this standard aims to close this gap by imposing standards which should be applied by the large insurance companies from 2021. this regulation is sensitive to companies, since the assessment of liabilities in insurance contracts, especially long-term contracts, will have an impact not only on the profit of insurers but also on the levels of capital required for prudent management to cope with any unforeseen event. it is no coincidence that the big lobbyists are already on the ground, criticizing the rule for the high costs of its implementation and its limited effects. they don't know what they're talking about. we naturally also have doubts about the capacity of these rules, which do not touch on the essential issue, namely the conflict between private interests and the interests of citizens.
for the gue/ngl group madam president, i should also like to begin by thanking the rapporteur. blockchain and distributed ledger technology is a promise that has been around for over a decade, but so far there's a lot of discussion about the infinite applications, and the ones that we know best are the applications in virtual currencies, for example, those in financial markets, and there what we've seen, in a way, is disastrous, fraud, extreme fluctuations of a safe haven for tax evasion and money laundering. blockchain and dlt, more than a significant technological innovation, are actually a social and organizational innovation because it's about dispensing with intermediaries, counterparties, and often even the state itself. now that we are thinking of applying to other sectors - such as energy, education and others that have already been mentioned here - i think we should also talk about the concerns and why we are asking for legislation and the need for legislation in this regard. how do we ensure transparency, how do we ensure data protection, how do we ensure regulation, if we want better public services? how can we have better public services and ensure that there is no elimination of what should be the purpose of those same public services? that is why legislation is needed to ensure that what happened in the financial system and with virtual currencies is not repeated in other sectors and in the promises that have existed for more than ten years. then yes, they can be a very useful tool for our society.
madam president, commissioner, blockchain technologies and progressive encryption applications are intended to increase trust, control and transparency in a set of processes that are increasingly complex and fundamental in the new digital society. but this impact is not automatic. the technological implications and their applications are not neutral and so we have a job to do. we need to ensure that new solutions based on digital innovation embody the values and civilizational principles that we have developed and share in the common european space. it is in this context that the questions put to the european commission by the rapporteur and my colleague eva kaili make perfect sense and require a thoughtful and rapid response. we need to be competitive on a global scale, also in these technologies, but this competitiveness cannot be achieved at any price. no, it's not true that it's all done. there's a lot of things we have to do. it must be developed within a framework of legal certainty and robustness in relation to the control of processes and the risks of misuse of power without proper legitimacy. the concept of a multi- and self-regulating process does not eliminate the risks of induced centralisation or bias. these risks exist and must be monitored and tackled proactively. data protection, cybersecurity, interoperability or fraud prevention are areas where the regulatory role and the role of ensuring multiple and transparent governance also have to be ensured by public authorities at their various levels. the dissemination of development protocols that promote decentralisation and transparent verification, the creation of the european observatory and the recently signed partnership are signs that we are moving in the right direction. a sense that allows the european union to be competitive and at the same time able to use these new platforms to promote social innovation in a decentralised, secure and in line with its values.

mr president, the creation of a treaty legally binding multinational companies to respect for human rights is a longstanding aspiration of civil society worldwide dating back to the 1970s. this is due to the insufficiency of the rules voluntarily adopted by undertakings on the one hand and the difficulty of clearly determining the jurisdiction of the states in the case of undertakings carrying on their activities in third countries on the other. globalisation has made this system more complex by weakening traditional borders and encouraging the geographical mobility of firms. the potential negative effects of multinational activities are of particular concern to the most vulnerable and unprotected communities, such as indigenous and peasant communities. in 2014, the establishment of a un intergovernmental working group to draft a binding treaty proposal led by ecuador and south africa gave new impetus to this long-standing aspiration. the activities of this group were, however, met with considerable scepticism. the truth is that there has not been the commitment and enthusiasm that would be expected from a number of actors, including the countries of the european union. nevertheless, this initiative is beginning to bear fruit with a first draft treaty prepared by ecuador. the major challenge is that the treaty covers the multiple situations of potential negative human rights impact that arise from business activity indirectly, via its branches, subsidiaries and contractors. it is on this dimension that the european union must concentrate its efforts and its contributions. we are convinced that this treaty can take us to a new level in regulating globalisation, and can pave the way for a responsible and sustainable globalisation, starting with guarantees of access to justice and redress. the european union, therefore, in fidelity to the values which inspire it, must aspire to play a decisive and even a leading role in this respect. we must therefore call on all member states to overcome their divisions on this treaty and to make a joint effort to adopt it.

mr president, i voted in favour of this report because i believe that health technology assessment is a key point of promotion and innovation, which can bring real health gains for patients and for society as a whole. it is on the basis of this evaluation that pricing and sharing can be established, which is of paramount importance for the sustainability of health systems. this proposal is balanced and based on the spirit of collaboration between the member states making up the union, but it still leaves room for manoeuvre for national decisions. centralization avoids duplication of assessments, saves resources and will create a less asymmetric europe in access to healthcare. although this is a meeting of the combined technical and scientific efforts of the national agencies, i welcome the inclusion of patient representatives in the evaluation process in the sense that the best value of a therapy is considered to go beyond mere clinical or pharmacological statistics, and the contribution of technology to the ease of treatment or to the quality of life of the patient can be considered. to this end, it is essential that the process is as transparent as possible in order to transmit trust throughout the entire value chain of the medicinal product.

rapporteur: mr president, commissioner bienkowska, it has been four years since the reform of public procurement legislation. it is time to take stock of its implementation by the member states. what is going well and what is going badly in the application of european rules. we agree that for the internal market to function well, it is crucial to define a comprehensive policy and strategic framework for public procurement that promotes growth, jobs and innovation. public procurement represents a substantial part of public investment in our economy. over two billion euros a year. this means that 250 000 public authorities spend around 14% of the union's gdp on the purchase of services, works and supplies through public contracts. we know that the delivery of high-quality public services depends to a large extent on modern, efficient and transparent public procurement processes. the report under discussion today responds precisely to this need. we agree with the european commission's strategy of proposing a package of non-legislative measures, two communications and a recommendation on the professionalisation of public procurement. we don't need more laws, but we need good implementation of the laws we have. we have therefore adopted a set of recommendations which the committee on the internal market wants to see implemented. ladies and gentlemen, the directives adopted in 2014 are not just a tool to regulate public procurement of goods, services and works. they should contribute to member states promoting policies of innovation, green and circular economy, sustainable and inclusive growth. we regret, however, the disappointing pace at which many member states have transposed the directives, even leading to infringement proceedings, and we are aware that many of the criticisms point to a protectionist approach, the difficult access and understanding for small and mediumsized enterprises, the lack of transparency and the fact that the only award criterion seems to be the lowest price, rather than, for example, social and environmental criteria which are strategic for the communities. the commission has already made a number of proposals in the past, and i am sure that the commission will continue to make proposals in the future. support the participation of small and medium-sized enterprises in public procurement, for example by encouraging the division of contracts into lots, paragraph 38. transparency instruments such as contract registers are introduced, paragraphs 29, 33 and 41. invest in and accelerate digitalisation at all stages of the public procurement process, paragraphs 26 to 33, and promote innovation, paragraphs 4, 23 and 24. develop national public procurement strategies, systematic data analysis and professionalisation plans for those dealing with public contracts, paragraphs 53 to 57. combating unfair competition in cross-border public procurement procedures and improving access to public procurement markets in third countries, paragraphs 45 to 49. if high ethical and deontological standards are applied at all stages of the process, paragraphs 15 and 20. in conclusion, mr president, i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs who have worked so hard on this report and on the opinions drawn up by the envi and intra committees. i believe we have come up with a good report, which sets out the right approach to efficient, effective and competitive public procurement, which responds to the challenges facing the sector today and which makes a strategic contribution to achieving objectives such as innovation, social inclusion and economic and environmental sustainability. i hope that all members will support the final adoption of this report, which promotes the proper functioning of the single market, the growth of businesses and employment and protects the interests of citizens. ensuring that taxpayers' money is spent efficiently and effectively is in the common interest and is a legitimate demand of our citizens.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group. mr president, public procurement is - and should be - an important instrument for states to defend and promote their productive fabric, quality and decent employment, environmental protection, among other strategic objectives. while the current legislative framework in the european union theoretically allows for very partial consideration of some of these concerns, it is in reality practically impossible to exploit the full potential of this instrument, since this legislative framework is subject to and constrained by the sacrosanct logic of free competition in the single market. in other words, the legislation is all tailored to the interests of the big companies, the big transnational corporations which effectively dominate the single market and which have long been converging on the public procurement market their voracious appetite which is not yet fully satisfied. this is coupled with the advance of the market in areas which should have been protected from it, particularly in the case of public services which have been progressively dismantled and privatised, thanks also to the action, legislation and pressure of the european union, precisely to expand the business of public procurement. answer according to the "blue card" procedure. very quickly, my dear colleague, i am indeed aware in my assessment here of the package of legislation that was adopted in 2014. despite all the rhetoric that permeates it in defence of small and mediumsized enterprises, as is the case with many other european union legislation, the truth is that it is tailored to the interests of large companies precisely because it is subject to this logic of free competition in the single market, because they are the ones who, in free competition in the single market, are effectively dominating that market and do not forget, do not forget, because it is very important, that the station is combined with the dismantling of public services in water, postal services, energy, as is now also being done in health and is being done in health to open these areas precisely to public procurement.
the rapporteur. - mr president, i would like to thank commissioner bienkowska for the references she has made and for what she has said. start by underlining exactly what she just said. we are faced with a clear case where europe has done well, where we have adopted our legislative framework on time and where the problems are the result of the slow and sometimes incorrect transposition of legislation by the member states. we can't ask for more from ourselves. we must ask the member states not to undermine what we have been able to do at european level. i would like to thank all colleagues for their contributions and underline three points: first, i think there is a great deal of consensus on the broad objectives of this initiative, the idea that with this instrument we will promote more transparency and fight corruption, we will ensure fair competition and we will have instruments to pursue the objectives of innovation, of green and circular economy and of sustainable and inclusive growth, the idea that here we also have to have social criteria. the second idea is concern for small and mediumsized enterprises. i think that was common to most of the speeches we heard this morning, and thirdly and lastly, the idea of digitisation, not only as a tool for simplifying processes but also for ensuring greater transparency. mr president, i believe that this is a concern of all of us, ensuring that taxpayers' money is well spent is a demand that all of our citizens make and i hope that in today's vote there will be a clear signal from the european parliament around the recommendations we are proposing.

madam president, i would like to congratulate my colleague carlos coelho on his brilliant work on the public procurement strategy and he stresses that spending a considerable amount of taxpayers' money has to be ethical, transparent and honest in order to efficiently deliver quality goods and services to citizens. according to the commission, 55% of public procurement procedures still use the lowest price as the only award criterion, without considering any social, economic sustainability or environmental protection criteria in these decisions. the seriousness of procurement processes requires an end to amateurism and the empirical management of procurement by public entities. finally, after four years, following the review of the four legislative acts, the commission calls for the modernisation and simplification of public procurement systems by using e-government technologies and the use of economies of scale, with cross-border joint procurement, making full use of the european single market. i cannot, however, fail to mention the consequences of irresponsible public management, as in the case of the public-private road partnerships in portugal, which have cost the state millions of euros. i salute the civil society organisations in my country who have been able to denounce these situations and try to obtain the justice they deserve. for the sake of taxpayers, i hope that member states will welcome this report and use it to bring about the necessary change in public management.
member of the commission. – madam president, i took this point on behalf of commissioner andriukaitis, but i have to tell you that i am personally very interested in the subject too. i am very concerned about animal welfare and so i am happy to be here with you. the eu is a major global producer, as was said, of broilers. the poultry sector employees over a quarter of a million people in europe, with 23 000 large broiler farms in europe. but of course for us the most important thing for now is discussing animal welfare, and bird welfare. over the years, there has been good work in this house on that issue, and i thank you very much for this. the european union has developed a range of legislation, which is leading the world on animal welfare. our farmers, in each of the 28 member states, put this legislation into force almost every day. we take animal welfare very seriously and remain fully committed to constant improvement through the life of the directive and other legislation. we work closely with the member states to ensure that the legislation is fully enforced. this includes the directive on the protection of broilers, the broiler directive. since coming into force in 2010, the broiler directive has provided a legal framework to the member states, which has considerably improved the management and housing of broilers. this in turn has improved the welfare of the birds. the broiler directive was the first piece of eu legislation to introduce animal—based indicators as a way to monitor the welfare of animals. current information and research shows that the use of these indicators, such as footpad dermatitis in poultry, has brought about the majority of improvements in farm welfare for chickens. encouraged by this, we will continue to develop animal—based indicators as a way of continually improving conditions for animals. we believe that there is still a lot of room for improvement in animal welfare, but the work already carried out by the european union gives us hope that a lot has also been achieved so far. in 2019, the commission, together with the member states and stakeholders, will work to identify further animal—based indicators for broilers. we hope that these new indicators can be used by national authorities and also by the commission to better target their action and thus improve the implementation of the directive. investment in the health and welfare of animals on farms, of course, brings additional benefits. as has just been mentioned, improvements in animal husbandry have reduced the need to use medication on farms, including antibiotics. this is very significant and provides a strong boost to our fight against antimicrobial resistance. through the marketing standards for poultry meat regulation, we provide incentives and support for animal—welfare—friendly chicken production. we list certain specific terms, which can only be used for poultry meat produced in alternative production systems. these include terms such as ‘extensive indoor’, ‘traditional free range’ and ‘total freedom free range’, which are defined and limited to farms meeting specific criteria. member states may additionally co—finance investment in animal—welfare—friendly production systems through the rural development programmes. this funding can be used, for example, to upgrade, fit and improve housing where it goes beyond the minimum required standards. as regards the question of mandatory labelling of poultry meat, the commission carried out a feasibility study on animal welfare labelling, following extensive stakeholder consultation, and the outcome of this study was included in a report from the commission to the european parliament and the council, adopted at the end of 2009. this study concluded that mandatory animal welfare labelling in respect of minimum eu standards would offer no added value. our work to improve animal welfare standards and implementation in the eu continues. in 2019, work will commence on the designation of the eu reference centre for animal welfare for poultry, rabbits and fur animals. this centre is expected to provide scientific and technical information, through which we will be able to support member states in the implementation of animal welfare rules. i look forward to the discussion and your views on this.
on behalf of the ppe group. madam president, commissioner, on 13 september we adopted the report also by my colleague karin kadenbach on the european commission's proposal for the european 'one health' action plan against antimicrobial resistance. i had the opportunity to say here what i say now: it is now essential to stop the rampant use of antibiotics introduced by intensified animal husbandry practices. commissioner, while we can accept that antibiotics are important in ensuring food security, we cannot allow the development of breeding technologies to reduce costs and achieve maximum yield in animal production to be at the expense of our future health. the truth is that high density farming uses antibiotics improperly and systematically on poultry farms, often in unsanitary environments, to prevent the spread of diseases resulting from the cramped, confined and stressful conditions in which the animals are kept. these conditions have the added disadvantage of inhibiting the natural immune system of animals, but the use of antibiotics such as prophylaxis and metaphylaxis in food-producing animals will see its ambitious end this year with the final approval of the report on the regulation on the use of veterinary medicinal products. it will also make it very clear that the disease is to be prevented not by the routine use of antimicrobials but by good hygiene practices or techniques of compartmentalisation and good animal management. i believe that protecting consumers and public health means using healthy, high-quality, genetically diverse breeding animals that grow naturally, ensuring breeding conditions that respect the behavioural needs of the species such as social interactions and hierarchies, and maintaining a density of animals that does not increase the risk of disease transmission. commissioner, i now ask the question which is implicit in my colleagues' oral question: i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has already taken the necessary steps to ensure that the member states have the necessary measures to ensure that quality food products are available on the market without unnecessary antibiotics to prevent the emergence of antimicrobial resistance.

pech reporter. - mr president, dear commissioner and dear friend, plastics are one of the great innovations of the 20th century with major impacts on the economy and on the daily lives of all of us. on the contrary, it has disastrous impacts on the environment, on biodiversity, on the health of ecosystems and, absurd and contradictory as it may seem, on the quality of everyday life. it is unfortunate that we have allowed plastics to expand to such an extent that we have only now actually started to produce governance instruments to correct the management errors of the last 70 years. let it serve as a warning not to neglect the guard and the precaution for management and governance with regard to current and future technological innovations. fishing accounts for 27% of single-use plastics that occur in the sea. it is therefore very important to harmonize this proposal with the directive on the reception of waste in port areas. the plastics directive is only a delay and i hope that an ambitious document will be obtained by wednesday.
mr president, commissioner, we talk about pollution in the oceans and the first thing that comes to mind is drifting plastic. plastics are a visible problem that we can no longer ignore, especially the avoidable accumulation in our oceans and on our beaches. the presence of the disposable for its ease of use has led to the unnecessary use of the use of this type of products. consumption has to be more sustainable and it goes through a greater consumer awareness, to the preference for reusable materials, to the correct way to dispose of waste and to the extreme need to use disposables that have an option for biodegradable materials safely in the aquatic environment. i therefore believe that the measures we are about to adopt will prevent the situation from worsening in the future, but they will not erase in a flash the problem we have today throughout the world. we need to find sustainable solutions, including investing in ocean cleanup through waste collection and recycling. plastic doesn't belong in the environment, nor in the landfill. complying with the circulation of the economy and taking the proper value of this waste to reduce its environmental footprint is essential. eventually, the rest of the world will follow our lead.

mr president, the structural funds are not, and have never been, an expression of supposed european solidarity. they are, and always have been, a monetary compensation for the asymmetrical impact of integration. if there are winners in the single market, the euro and common policies, there are also losers, and some lose a lot. hence this form of compensation, which is insufficient and in the interest of the member states, and it is in the light of this reality that the proposals for regulations on the structural funds put forward by the european commission are extremely worrying. if these proposals are not changed, it will be more difficult to use the least available money, it will mean that the use of these funds will not be subject to the interests and needs of each state, especially those facing the greatest difficulties, but subject to the political objectives set by the major powers in defence of their own interests, those of their multinationals, including in the area of the arms industry. that is why they want to pass these regulations quickly, without public involvement and discussion. that is why we must denounce and combat this intention.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, commissioner, there is no doubt today that interference through social networks (we are looking at facebook here, but also at other networks), manipulation of the political point of view and of a more general point of view are a danger, a risk, a threat. the cambridge analytica case has clearly shown that it is possible to interfere in an election process. we are not concerned only with the electoral question; the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the data are not used for political purposes. and so, based on this case that we know, based on the strong suspicions that we have in the case of the united states, based, for example, on what is precisely happening now in brazil, where the suspicions of fake news and manipulation of algorithms to campaign for elections are multiplying from side to side, it is essential that we take measures, measures for the european elections because these are an emergency, but measures that are medium and long-term for all electoral processes. as i said, for us it is not just an electoral issue that is at stake, it is a question of general citizenship for all subjects. this means that it will not be just with an interference or with legislation in this case, but by understanding how we can look at the democratic society of the future, knowing that social networks have to have a role in it and that we, just as we were able to regulate the role of the media in the past society, will be able to regulate the role of the new world of social networks in a modern democratic society. the challenge before us is a constitutional challenge far more ambitious than simply preventing interference for the time being.
mr. president, in 2013, snowden revealed to us how the us agencies had put the biggest companies in the internet sector to spy on users. the world then realized how fragile their privacy and the protection of their data was. five years later, the cambridge analytica scandal has shown us how on the edge of the cliff, what was decided was to take a step forward. privacy and our personal data has become one of capitalism's most valuable assets, and we now know that personal data has been used as a weapon in political combat. add to that the growth of fake news, lies and hate speech, and you have the perfect recipe for mass election manipulation. platforms like facebook and whatsapp do not have the capacity or transparency to handle the information they offer, let alone to protect it, as we see in brazil. so i ask what more needs to happen to continue defending our democracies and to defend democratic elections.
mr president, the use of personal data, whether voluntarily or involuntarily, by the social network facebook, or by other networks and platforms, to manipulate political choices and options and mass behaviour, raises a civilizational question. values such as privacy, transparency, trust and ultimately the freedom of choice of citizens are at stake. as we prepare to make the huge investment in europe's digital agenda, we need to set an ethical and legal standard applicable to all companies operating in, or interacting with, our territory, and develop robust tools for checking and sanctioning those who do not. at the same time, it is important to progressively develop a social media management model closer to the internet model, more multi-stakeholder and less based on the big hegemonic corporate powers. you have to give back to society what's rightfully its own. we must put technological development at the service of individuals and not of those who want to manipulate them and distort their freedom of choice.

madam president, i voted in favour of this resolution because of the urgency of setting minimum standards for water quality in the union and ensuring that all citizens of the european union have access to this fundamental good. access to safe drinking water must be considered a fundamental human right. just because water is essential to human health, it cannot be enslaved to the distribution business. as a public good, i advocate standards of quality and waste prevention that are only achievable through the ethical exploitation of water. these are the values that must be promoted when we talk about the efficiency of distribution systems. i would also stress that in 2010 the united nations declared water to be a human right, alongside sanitation, and that access to drinking water should not be conditioned by the lack of economic resources of the end user. that is why i voted in favour of providing drinking water points in public places, of controlled quality, as they already exist in many member states on a voluntary basis, in the hope that it will also become the rule throughout the european union.

this cumex scandal, another one, is intolerable to all intents and purposes. eur 55 billion was withheld from tax administrations between 2012 and 2015. in other words, while eu governments were ruthlessly falling on taxpayers to recapitalise the banks, the banks themselves were creating tax evasion schemes by trading shares with non-residents in order to avoid taxation of dividends. this case once again reveals what we've been fighting for. a genuine fight against tax fraud and tax evasion necessarily involves questioning and tampering with the two sacred cows of this european union: the financial system and the free movement of capital. in this sense, public control of banking and control of capital movements is now a matter of urgency.

the commission is also aware of the fact that the european parliament has been involved in the negotiations on the schengen agreement since the beginning of the year. for all this, we need to improve the schengen information system. we have spent about six months in interinstitutional negotiations on sis, half the time we spent on the previous reform. in the past half year we have fought for more security, more freedom, more schengen for the citizens of europe. the reform of the schengen information system is adapting the system to the challenges posed by migration, responding to the new threats of terrorism and organised crime, taking advantage of technological developments and strengthening respect for fundamental rights, in particular data protection. at a time when the european union is accused of failing to respond in time to the needs of its citizens, the implementation of this reform will begin as early as the beginning of next year. for the first time, we have a deadline for the reform to come into force, and we have mechanisms to ensure greater control and transparency of costs. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial interests are protected. member states and the commission have to meet deadlines and budgets. within the cost schedule, the new sis must be fully operational. the impact of this reform is profound. the new schengen information system will change the way border police authorities share information across europe. we don't need more information, we need better information. let's better protect our children, not just when the worst has happened and we don't know where they are, but through new alerts for children at risk. we will be able to prevent these abductions or such dramatic realities as genital mutilation, but we will also improve the safety of everyone through closer monitoring of so-called foreign fighters and other criminals, through new means of information gathering, inquiry checks, through better biomedical data such as fingerprints and facial images. it will not only be possible to search for these elements, but also to search throughout europe for unknown perpetrators of crimes whose fingerprints have been found at the scene of the crime. we will improve controls at our external borders. we have carried out the biggest review of schengen entry bans ever. it will be virtually impossible for anyone refused entry by portugal to enter germany, but certain requirements will have to be met. we improve the responsiveness of the system, increase its processing capacity and strengthen its defenses against potential attacks, security problems or simple failures. and we call on the member states to be more effective. the response times of the sirene bureaux have been shortened and requests for information in cases concerning children or terrorism require an immediate response. last but not least, we have put sis at the heart of european action, providing access to frontex and europol, and further increasing the value of their action. i am confident, mr president, that tomorrow this house will approve without reservation the third reform of the schengen information system. i believe that more europe, more sis, allows for better information and better coordination, allows for more security and more freedom.
mr president, i should like to begin by congratulating the rapporteurs, and in particular mr carlos coelho, who has made an extraordinary contribution over the past few years in the european parliament to finding balanced and fair solutions in such a difficult sector. those who think that freedom can be guaranteed in a context of insecurity are sorely mistaken. there is no better breeding ground for the emergence of tyrannical solutions than a framework of insecurity. insecurity often generates a desire for excessive protection which immediately leads to the accreditation of tyrannies, and therefore we must have the notion that a free society must also be a secure society and we must strike a balance between these two dimensions, between the dimension of protecting the security of citizens and the dimension, of course, of respecting the fundamental rights of those same citizens. the work that has been carried out here is precisely in that direction: the modernisation of an essential instrument, the sis system, which is the fundamental instrument for ensuring the strengthening of security, particularly at the external borders, and at the same time safeguarding the fundamental rights of european citizens. we have nothing but reason to be satisfied with this work.

the european council followed the pattern of other recent summits. its members addressed issues of great impact on the daily lives of people and businesses, but the absence of decisions capable of conveying confidence and strategic clarity made it another moment that will go down in history as an example of the lack of ambition in the european union's response to the challenges it faces. i would like to stress the progress made in the brexit negotiations, safeguarding the basic principles on which the process has been developed, protecting the rights of european citizens, pushing for a border model that does not undermine ireland's full membership and establishing the lines of force that can lead to future partnerships between the european union and the united kingdom. i also note the intention expressed to reach agreement by the end of the year on banking union and to continue the technical studies for the necessary reform of economic and monetary union. the council also took important steps in terms of internal security and the control of migration flows, but it was still notorious for its lack of an integrated and humanistic view of the threats and opportunities posed to the european union by the new geostrategic framework.
mr president, i also know the lisbon treaty quite well because i was part of the team of the portuguese presidency in charge of completing the negotiation of this treaty in lisbon back in 2007. let me make a clear point. the problem we have is not with the lisbon treaty. the problem we have is with the kind of policies we need to implement in order to deliver better european solutions. let’s be fair: when we focus on the treaty, the treaty has involved important progress when it comes to democracy. first of all, the fact that this parliament has extended co—decision in many, many new areas. this is really deepening european democracy. second, the lisbon treaty could pool sovereignty where this is needed: when we address climate change; when we negotiate trade agreements; when we affirm our monetary sovereignty. in all these areas we need stronger european sovereignty. this does not go against national sovereignty. this is to strengthen our control of the situation. i am now addressing the part of parliament and those members of the european parliament who are taking a position against the european union and the treaties of the european union. you are wrong. this is not the problem, and the solution is not to withdraw from the treaties. the solution is to change policies in favour of more economic and social convergence and more tax fairness. that’s for sure. so this is the way to go.
mr president, commissioner, in the context of the evaluation of the lisbon treaty, a conclusion that stands out: we are far from the promises and hopes which he embodied. to quote a portuguese poet, the european union is not yet fulfilled. if integration has advanced in the economy with the single market and in its political configuration as well, albeit with hesitations and contradictions, in the fields of justice and the courts, which are the other pole of the binomial democratic rule of law, nothing has been overlooked. justice, especially criminal justice, is too important a value in the process of european integration and cohesion to be left to the exclusive care of the member states. a state is only a state governed by the rule of law if it is a democratic state and it is only democratic if it is also a state governed by the rule of law. it is therefore urgent to transform eurojust into a general prosecutor's office of the union, and to set up a european court with exclusive jurisdiction to try a catalogue of crossborder crimes in the european union.
mr president, it should be remembered that the treaty of lisbon, adopted during the portuguese presidency in 2007, was an important step in the construction of europe, which overcame several years of institutional impasse, made it possible to value european citizenship and fundamental rights, strengthen the european parliament and democracy and simplify the decision-making processes of our institutions. i had the honour of participating in the european council that approved the treaty of lisbon and in the signing ceremony that took place in the monastery of the hieronymites. not all the problems have been solved, but i also know how difficult it was to reach that political agreement. so, ten years on, rather than a new complicated process of treaty reform, what we need is to harness the unused potential of the lisbon treaty to improve the functioning of our institutions and strengthen the european project. the lisbon treaty is not the problem, it remains part of the solution.
mr president, the present productivist model, which has been imposed on our farmers and livestock producers, puts us in a permanent health alert situation. they're new diseases that are emerging or old diseases that there are no effective drugs against. the regulation identifies the problem but deliberately omits the role of the multinationals, which are always seeking to maximise their monopoly income by discriminating against markets or deliberately delaying innovation. we agree with the principle of centralised authorisation, because it will limit the possibility of discriminating against markets, but we understand that support for innovation cannot be limited to giving more favors to multinationals by extending the duration of patents. veterinary research is crucial for the future of agriculture and this requires greater support or public research centres, so that science is at the service of agriculture and not at the service of some multinational companies.
mr president, the package of measures we are discussing here today is of enormous importance. the growing resistance to antibiotics is a huge threat. its infections kill at least 50,000 people a year in europe and the united states and 700,000 people worldwide. these numbers are expected to increase dramatically over the next 30 years with a projected 10 million deaths per year by 2050. a number that will exceed the deaths from cancer and diabetes unless we act, and i commend the rapporteurs, the rapporteurs, for the work they do. the limitation of the prophylactic use of microbial agents in animals will benefit from the establishment of community authorisation and monitoring procedures. the allocation of specific powers to the european medicines agency to authorise products for veterinary use through a centralised procedure is also very important. the strengthening of the regulatory framework for the manufacture, authorisation and distribution of veterinary medicinal products is crucial. that is the direction we must continue to work in.
mr president, commissioner, antimicrobial resistance and the consequent ineffectiveness of treatments in the fight against disease is one of the greatest public health challenges we face today. the agricultural and livestock sectors have an increased responsibility in combating this challenge. this will have to go through the prevention and control of infections and the reduction of the need for the use of antibiotics through the application of high animal welfare standards. antibiotics should under no circumstances be used as a substitute for these standards, but the success of this fight also requires a commitment to research and innovation in order to stimulate the development of new antimicrobial agents, alternative therapies and diagnostics that contribute to reducing the use of antibiotics. the incentives for this research should be clearly present in our priorities. finally, i should like to congratulate our colleague, mrs clara aguilera, on her report.
mr president, promoting the use of clean and energy-efficient road transport vehicles is a priority, but the priority requires that public authorities take on increased responsibility for the use of road transport. they have to be an example and they have to be drivers for the implementation of smart, clean and inclusive mobility systems. the 75% target for the minimum percentage of clean vehicles to be included in public procurement by 2030 and the incentives for the use of zero-emission shelters are very important. first, to improve the air quality in cities by creating better living conditions and healthier environments. secondly, to reduce greenhouse gas emissions in line with the targets set in the paris agreement and, finally, to boost european industry towards the technological transition by creating more and better working conditions. but let us be pragmatic, commissioner, this priority, if it is to be a priority, must be reflected in the allocation of financial resources to the various cohesion, convergence and competitiveness programmes, which are essential to support the transition and to build the necessary infrastructure.
mr president, commissioner, with the bologna process and the reforms in higher education we have achieved greater recognition of qualifications between member states. what made our system fragile, which was the comparability of degrees with third countries, seems to have been solved. however, we have created a new problem in that some member states have not made the transition and have failed to equalise training between the pre-bologna past and the new post-bologna present. we cannot have member states with public calls for proposals requiring post-bologna training and leaving out citizens who have completed the same equivalent level of curriculum in the pre-bologna educational framework and in some cases even with more years of study and more ects. we talk about automatic recognition of diplomas between member states, but we seem to have forgotten to reconcile recognition with our recent past. at the level of transnational cooperation, using information and communication technologies, the recognition of qualifications of origin for access to regulated professions is done through the online platform of the internal market information system. you don't have to invent the wheel to do the same across the education system. i do not want to say that preparing future generations requires harmonizing education at european level. the diversity of curricula makes it possible to prepare citizens who are better adapted to the challenges of each member state and gives us greater adaptability to face the future. what is needed is for member states to agree on educational compliance targets with mutual recognition of the award of a degree, without this entailing the ratification of the content. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the mastery of the national languages remains its greatest obstacle.
president-in-office of the council. – mr president, on the issue at hand concerning the lack of implementation of the reach regulation and, as a consequence, the extensive use of non-tested chemicals in the eu, the presidency and the council as a whole acknowledged the sentiments expressed by certain members of this house, as well as the commitment of the other institutions to addressing this issue. the presidency takes note of the report from the german federal institute for risk assessment, which has alerted us to the risks deriving from placing on the market potentially harmful chemicals without correctly assessing whether they are mutagenic, teratogenic or toxic. the presidency thanks members for drawing our attention to these risks and the magnitude of the problem. we also take note of the fact that the european chemicals agency (echa) agrees with the report’s overall assessment, confirming that the quality of data on chemicals in registration dossiers still needs to improve. this view is also shared by the presidency. in the 10 years since reach entered into force, safety in the eu has undoubtedly increased, but there is still much to do. both industry and the authorities must increase their efforts to improve the quality of safety information on chemicals. it should be stressed though that the council’s role in this process has recently been very limited as no new legislation in the area of reach has been proposed since the mid—term review in 2013. echa’s enforcement forum is currently designing tailored enforcement actions, together with member states, to increase compliance of the dossiers concerned. in instances such as these, it is to the commission, in its role as guardian of the treaties and as enforcer of union level legislation, that we should turn for guidance and action that is appropriate and justified. on that note, if you allow me, i would like to draw your attention to the second reach review, which was discussed in the council bodies earlier this year. this report showed that, in the 10 years since its entry into force, reach has become fully operational and is delivering results towards achieving its stated objectives. we should not lose sight of the progress made. the report, however, also identifies priorities and actions needed to further improve the application of the reach regulation. among these, it was noted that member states identified the quality of registration dossiers as the main issue that needs to be addressed in a timely manner. member states expressed concerns about two things; firstly, that some registration dossiers do not comply with the information requirement that the reach regulation lays down, and, secondly, how updates to registration dossiers can be ensured. as to time, in june of this year, the commission expressed its intention to work with the european chemicals agency and the member states to identify the reasons for the poor quality of dossiers’ updates. the commission also signalled that it would make the appropriate proposals for improvements to this system by the first quarter of 2019. the presidency is launching discussions on this issue in the council working groups concerned. moreover, the presidency is convening an international conference on the future chemicals policy in october in vienna and another one on green chemistry in november. the presidency will provide a progress report to the competitiveness council, as well as to the environmental council. the presidency would like to join the calls on the european chemicals agency, as well as on member states, to show that they are serious about protecting human health and the environment. we call on both industry and the authorities to increase their efforts on improving the quality of registration dossiers and, of course, the council also looks forward to any proposal that the commission may present in the future. i would like to conclude for now by expressing my gratitude for this opportunity to join in your discussion here today. i look forward to hearing your views and to conveying them back to my colleagues in the council.
member of the commission. – mr president, i am representing commissioner vella, who unfortunately cannot be here this evening. reach is the most comprehensive chemical legislation in the world. it was designed to address the knowledge gap on the chemicals manufactured and used in europe and to speed up risk reduction strategies for those substances that are harmful for health and harmful for the environment. the basic condition of reach is no data, no market. this means that companies must register the chemicals they make or import. without registration, the substance is not allowed on the market. in these registration dossiers, the companies must detail the impact of the chemicals on human health and the environment, and how to manage those risks. prior to registration, the european chemicals agency assesses all dossiers for completeness. the chemicals agency and the member states’ competent authorities also carry out targeted evaluations to check that the dossiers comply with the legal requirements. the commission is aware of recent reports indicating that an estimated one—third of the registration dossiers are non—compliant. the need to improve compliance of registration dossiers was one of the main findings of the second review carried out earlier this year, and specific actions are already being taken to address this issue. for example, we have asked and tasked the european chemicals agency to increase the efficiency of the evaluation procedures by 2019. we would like to stress that already now when a dossier is found to be non—compliant, it must be remediated by the respective company and brought into compliance. the reach regulation requires the chemicals agency to check the compliance of at least 5% of all registration dossiers. the agency does this by targeted compliance checks of the registration dossiers that matter most through its so-called integrated regulatory strategy. we are also looking at incentives to companies to regularly review the registration dossiers and update them when necessary. we should not lose sight of the fact that the reach review confirmed that, overall, reach is functioning and meeting its objectives, notwithstanding what i have just said. by the last registration deadline in may this year, more than 21 000 substances in 90 000 registration dossiers were registered at the european chemicals agency. this is a large amount of information unique in the world and it represents much more and better information on substances that are on the eu market, in 10 years. the reach review also confirmed that reach improved risk management of chemicals by companies as well as communication and safety along the supply chains. reach has also led to the listing of 191 substances of very high concern on the candidate list. this is in addition to the 68 entries on the restrictions list which represents hundreds of hazardous substances that are now banned. altogether, this means that hazardous chemicals are being replaced by safer alternatives. the evaluation procedure will help identify more substances of concern, i have no doubt. enforcement is, of course, of huge importance and this is the responsibility of member states. coordination of their activities through the enforcement forum is proving reasonably satisfactory at eu and national level. based on the findings of the reach review, i strongly believe that reach has already contributed to the protection of eu citizens’ health and the environment, but there is much more work needed to be done to improve implementation. i am calling on the constructive cooperation of all parties involved to improve the effectiveness of our legislation.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, commissioner, it is thanks to reach that europe has one of the most extensive chemical databases in the world, but progress over the last ten years has not been fast enough or transparent enough and the challenges we had in 2009 in assessing the two years of the opening of the agency are the same as we have today. it should be noted that of the 40 000 registrations, only four have been rejected, which is much less than the rejections before the implementation of reach. of course, we must take into account that echa only has to carry out follow-up checks on about 5% of the dossiers, yes, only 5%. the commission will think that the reach procedure is a success because it imposes barriers to entry through market access requirements and that is why there are only four rejections, but no. the evaluation carried out by the german federal institute for risk assessment and the german environment agency concluded that the number of substances complying with the criteria for biotic and abiotic grading of bioaccumulation and ecotoxicity never exceeded 50%. furthermore, the evaluation of this institute considered that the standard requirements are not well applied in the dossiers because they use experimental data or parallel data, or do not provide full justification for the absence of data. chemicals need to be taken more seriously and echa has a greater responsibility for its evaluation. one third of the large-volume chemicals manufactured or imported into the union since 2010 do not comply with the rules aimed at protecting health and the environment. we are talking about mutagenic substances with environmental toxicity that affect biodiversity and with human toxicity that can induce health problems such as metabolic, oncological and endocrine diseases. the commission is certainly aware that there are other dangerous chemicals which we use regularly with a much tighter legal mesh, medicines, but here the collaboration between member states is much greater. the regulation does not have to be the same for chemicals and pharmaceuticals, but to protect public health. we need european agencies which can act as competent authorities and which are impervious to the interests of market self-regulation.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, according to a study recently published by the german environment agency, since 2010 one third of chemicals produced or imported into the european union have violated european standards to protect public health or the environment. only 31% of the chemicals investigated were declared legally compliant. many companies are therefore in breach of reach by not informing the european chemicals agency whether the substances they are marketing are carcinogenic or harmful. millions of tons of these substances have been dumped into the production of all kinds of industrial and consumer goods, from paint, packaging, furniture to building materials. exposure to many types of industrial chemicals, mainly through skin respiration, is a major cause of the increasing rates of metabolic diseases, hormonal cancers and neurological diseases. the un special rapporteur on hazardous substances and wastes has warned that incessant exposure to toxic substances can create a silent pandemic of disease. that is why it is urgent to change this scenario. concrete actions are needed to verify and strengthen the implementation of reach. we must put into practice the principle 'no data, no market', i.e. put an end to the automatic granting of non-compliant or insufficient registrations. we have adequate and world-class health and environmental protection rules. that is why we must ensure that it is implemented.
madam president, the regulation of the use of medical devices and in vitro diagnostic medical devices, based on the definition of design and manufacturing rules, clinical trials, authorization, marketing and post-market surveillance, has proved incapable of preventing the circulation of low-quality devices on our market, with serious consequences for users, and has revealed serious shortcomings in the legislative system. it is therefore important to highlight the work done in the european parliament on this subject, which has given rise to several proposals to improve the functioning, reliability and safety of these devices. it is true that some member states have reacted less enthusiastically to the definition of shared regulatory frameworks because there is effectively a legislative reservation in health matters. however, the european commission and the european parliament's dedicated work in dialogue, which is always essential with the member states, has made it possible to take significant steps in cooperation in the fields of health quality promotion, which have, as in this case, a clearly cross-border dimension. the strengthening of the regulation of in vitro diagnostic medical devices to ensure such important tests as pregnancy tests or blood tests is precise, must be accurate and reliable and sets a good example where such cooperation between member states is important and needs to be further strengthened. the regulatory framework, which entered into force on 25 may 2017, with a transition period of 3 and 5 years respectively, ensures good quality of these devices and consequent protection of patients. however, during this transposition period, difficulties in implementing the system have been identified, in particular in making cooperation between member states and the european bodies operational. the issue of the certification of medical devices in the united kingdom is also relevant, as after its withdrawal from the european union, all notified bodies based in the united kingdom will no longer be able to carry out conformity assessments in line with european legislation. health promotion is legally an intergovernmental competence, but the best interests of citizens require partnerships and concerted responses. that is why the questions put to the commission are of great importance and the answers deserve our full attention and follow-up.
