(the speaker accepts the question on the 'blue card' (rule 162 (8)).
blue-card answer. – i don’t think that we need to reduce the power of national states. what we need to do is to strengthen this power by combining it with the power of the european union in order to get the necessary resources to finance our budgets. a very good example is digital taxation. yes, we need to use the full power of the european union to impose on the big operators of the digital economy their duty to pay their taxes in the single market. like that, we can finance better public services at national level. so europe is a must for the power of national states and not a way to reduce it.
madam president, this proposal for a multiannual financial framework, with cuts in the common agricultural policy, cuts in cohesion policy and an increase in the security and defence headings, is not an accident of course. it is part of a continuity that comes from the past and reveals, in an eloquent way, the genetic code of this european union and its liberal and militarist matrix. this proposal for a mff is not good for portugal or for europe. in addition to the cuts which include support for the outermost regions of the azores and madeira, we will have a strengthening of macroeconomic conditionality, tying cohesion policy to the european semester. we shall see a sharp increase in the co-financing of the national states and we shall also see a greater predominance of financial instruments. in other words, far from offsetting the impacts of the single market and euro policies on the peripheral economies, this proposal will reinforce the asymmetries between countries, strengthening the richest at the expense of the poorest. this is the solidarity of the european union.
in writing. the proposal for the multiannual financial framework (mff) 2021-2027 presented by the european commission is profoundly negative for portugal and for europe. by cutting back on cohesion and the common agricultural policy, and increasing the 'security' and 'defence' headings, this proposal will accentuate the asymmetries between countries, doubly penalising peripheral economies that are being badly shaken by the asymmetrical impact of the single market, common eu policies and the euro and their instruments of economic governance. in addition to the cuts, the proposal for the mff contains other unacceptable aspects, among which we highlight a significant increase in national participation in the implementation of the structural funds, a greater predominance of financial instruments, a clear strengthening of macroeconomic conditionality, seeking to link the implementation of the structural funds to the european semester, and the creation of new forms of political conditionality. we have tabled dozens of amendments to the mff and to the european parliament's position, aimed at strengthening cohesion, eliminating the cap and posei cuts and reversing the expected sharp increase in military expenditure, including on armaments development, and the impetus for the ongoing security drift. the question is not when the mff will be approved (before or after may 2019), but whether it will be acceptable for portugal.

president-in-office of the council. – mr president, honourable members, dear commissioner, i consider the discussion we had as very useful and i would like to thank you for sharing your thoughts and also your concerns very openly. to conclude i would only like to recall one thing, and it was mentioned by some of you: there is no common position of the eu as such.
member of the commission. – mr president, the commission has been carefully following the work on the report on european humanitarian visas, prepared by the rapporteur, mr lópez aguilar. we take note of the calls on the commission to make a proposal on a european humanitarian visa by the end of march 2019. we also believe that in order to better manage migration comprehensively, we need to enhance legal and safe avenues to the eu for third-country nationals in need of international protection. increasing resettlement from turkey or niger is part of the solution to engaging with third countries and reducing irregular arrivals. at the same time, we believe that the greatest potential for reaching this common objective lies, at this point in time, in the swift adoption of the union resettlement framework regulation proposed by the commission in july 2016. it will be the first legislative instrument of its kind to better coordinate member states’ resettlement efforts, which until now have been the result of a combination of national or international programmes. i commend the efforts of parliament that have contributed greatly towards reaching a common position within the council. but both co-legislators need to take the last steps to transform the preliminary agreement into the final adoption of the proposal. the commission stands ready to provide all the support needed. we believe it is of utmost importance not to risk complicating the adoption of the union resettlement framework regulation, which now requires our full attention. it seems therefore appropriate that the commission includes in the evaluation of the future union resettlement framework regulation an assessment of whether or not it covers, as intended, the main legal and safe avenues for international protection in the eu.
the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. mr president, i take over for my colleague malin björk, who cannot be here today. approximately 90% of those receiving international protection in the european union arrive by irregular means. since 2000, 30,000 people have died crossing borders; 17,000 in the mediterranean since 2014 alone. in the eu, some 1 000 km of walls have been built. the european union cannot discharge itself of the causes of this very serious humanitarian crisis. instead of solidarity, it imposes greater restrictions on the application for international protection. asylum seekers are forced to risk their lives to reach safety, placing themselves in the hands of human trafficking networks. women, children and lgbt people are the most vulnerable at any stage of the migration process, have the most difficulty in accessing protection tools and are particularly subject to physical, psychological and sexual violence, often dragged into networks of sexual exploitation and slave labour. humanitarian aid, although insufficient, must be an agile response that provides the mechanisms, structures and technical and human resources necessary to protect those involved, with particular attention to the most vulnerable - women and children.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the code of conduct. i would like to highlight a few, among them the independence of national regulatory authorities, a more effective and regulated electronic communications system (this is fundamental), greater guarantees for consumers, especially with regard to communications on tariffs related to communications between different countries, higher speed and 5g, but we also need to ensure equal access, we need to ensure that all this reaches all citizens. there are also dimensions relating, for example, to the issues of updating telecommunications rules and the response that needs to be given to connectivity between different citizens and in different countries. we are talking about a package which certainly seeks to modernise the current telecommunications rules, but none of this can be done on the basis of competition alone. the need for regulation has already been mentioned here and i would like to draw attention to another issue which i consider to be fundamental: and so, communications is a very sensitive area, and so it's not just about the coding infrastructure that we're talking about, but also what the threats to the maintenance and management of communications might be. we must therefore ensure that the public sector can always buy back the basic networks for the provision of communications services, such as emergency management networks, civil protection or audiovisual signals. this is fundamental to preventing some disasters from happening in our communications.
madam president, commissioner, the stated political objective of this telecommunications legislative package is to create the large and liberalised european telecommunications market. the european union's electronic communications regulatory body, which the european commission wanted to give agency status, was set up to help shape the technical and political guidelines for the completion of this large open and liberalised market. the contradictions between the interests of the member states, especially some of them, and those of those who want a fully liberalised communications sector, putting aside what they consider to be inconvenient national interests, are obvious. it should be remembered that this was a public sector, dominated by public enterprises in several european countries, public because it was strategic and, because it was strategic, public must be again. the damage caused by privatisation and liberalisation to the people, to national interests, to the public interest is clearly visible; the benefits are only for the multinationals in the sector, which promote and benefit from monopolistic concentration on a european scale. this is a path that should be reversed and not pursued. (the speaker accepts the answer to a question on the 'blue card' (rule 162 (8)).
answer according to the "blue card" procedure. mrs kumpula-natri, i thank you for your question and what i can answer you, from the experience of my country, portugal, is how the privatisation of a strategic company that dominated the sector, that was at the forefront of innovation and that fulfilled a set of public interest missions, called into question the public interest and, in practice, led to the dismantling of this company, which is now dominated by a large french telecommunications group. that is what the liberalization advocated by you and other groups means. it is losing public control over a strategic sector of the economy, to the detriment of the national interest, the people, the consumers, the businesses and the public interest.
the european union must prepare to take advantage of the ongoing digital revolution to affirm a common vision of technological development at the service of citizens. the paris call, signed on 12 december by 51 countries and hundreds of companies and organisations, and to which the countries of the european union have enthusiastically joined, should be a reference for the implementation of the telecom package in terms of infrastructure development, regulation and access. the package discussed today proposes an integrated approach, flexible but with appropriate levels of assurance and predictability to make business investment viable. the commission has also adopted a proposal for a regulation on the protection of the euro against counterfeiting and for a regulation on the protection of the euro against counterfeiting. a system of warning in the event of natural disasters or terrorist attacks, which is mandatory throughout the union, is also established. i would also stress that the wifi4eu initiative, as part of the telecoms package, is key to enhancing equity in european citizens' access to high quality internet and its success and quality of implementation should be a priority for the european commission.
madam president, when i was a member of the european parliament's committee of inquiry into car emissions, there was one number that particularly struck me. four hundred thousand european citizens die prematurely every year because of poor air quality. therefore, decarbonising transport is a citizens' imperative for economic, environmental and public health reasons. heavy goods vehicles are responsible for 6% of total carbon dioxide emissions in the eu. we need to have a significant share of new, low-emission heavy-duty vehicles to reduce emissions in this sector by 35%. it is a challenge for european industry to develop, produce and market zero- and low-emission heavy-duty vehicles in europe and beyond. research in this field should be encouraged and supported, but it is not enough to support industry. a high percentage of freight transport operators in the european union are smes. it is also essential to provide a robust incentive structure to support them. we can't be left behind. we must aim to lead in innovative technologies for reducing emissions in transport to meet the paris agreement, but above all to improve the quality of life of european citizens.
mr president, i voted in favour of this report once again after the committee on the environment had adopted by a large majority the best compromise, which simultaneously advocates convergence with the ecological targets set by the paris agreement and the protection of social interests and which, moreover, also brings economic benefits. this is the true win-win solution. we are at a time when we cannot afford to fail to make commitments. i therefore reiterate the importance of introducing penalties for failure to meet co2 emission targets, alongside incentive measures for zero-emission vehicles. these penalties must be effective and cannot be set at less than the cost of the technological transition itself. if we do not, we will end up with the ridiculous idea that it is cheaper to pay fines than to make the necessary investments. we have to be consistent. the co2 correction factor for alternative high-performance fuels is already covered by the renewable energy directive ii. this kind of measure would lead to double benefit, which makes no sense in this regulation, since car manufacturers would be benefiting from an effort which is, in fact, made by the producers of these fuels. fellas, there is only one future... i think it is important, first of all, to realise that in the digital world, or as it was called before in the virtual world, the threats to transparency, to the principles of immunity and independence in electoral processes, if you will, to the pillars of democracy, are not virtual threats, they are not digital threats, they are real threats. and so it's critical that, in light of the experience of the last few years and particularly what is suspected to have happened in the case of brexit and the brexit referendum, which may have happened in the 2016 us elections, which may be happening in some movements, such as, for example, the movements regarding the question of catalonia and whether there should be more autonomy, less autonomy, independence or non-independence. what we find is that, digitally, there is constant interference in these processes and interference whose author we are unable to identify and often whose design we are unable to identify. and so it is with this new challenge to the issues of immunity, impartiality and transparency in the electoral process, but also in the debate which precedes the electoral process, that we have to review the procedures of public bodies and, in particular, of the bodies responsible for the electoral process, whether at national or european level. the first and most important point is data protection. we have already taken an important step when we have set up the framework through this regulation for data protection in which the european union is highly innovative. it is true that the commission itself has already put forward a number of ideas and proposals to combat cyberattacks, but we may have to go further. we have to go further and look at these threats as, i would say, global threats. and here i would like to introduce one more factor before i say which are the two areas in which i think we should act. we're dealing with external threats here. that is the subject of this current debate: external threats. but let's not kid ourselves, there are also internal threats. i mean, in this digital world, whether it's cyberattacks, whether it's the misuse of the data that's collected, there can be both internal agents and external agents, because the adversaries of democracy, the adversaries of transparency, the adversaries of exemption are not just foreign powers. we have evidence that they are acting in their geostrategic interest, but there is no doubt that there are also groups and entities within our borders, within the borders of europe, that have the same designs or that have designs of the same kind. and here i would say that we must act in two fundamental areas: one is the area of regulation of data processing and the protection of citizens in general and their data, and this goes to operators in general and also goes to companies, foundations, political parties, public authorities. and then we have to have another concern, which is a concern already of a criminal nature. it's a second concern, because no matter how many rules and safeguards we have, there will always be cyberattacks, there will always be criminals who have some success. and we must have technological conditions and police investigative conditions that allow us also to prevent and repel and punish these kinds of attacks, whether they are foreign attacks, whether they are domestic attacks.
mr president, politics has always been accompanied by information and counter-information strategies. today, however, the technological association allows for much more and promotes massive disinformation campaigns, designed to target and manipulate specific sectors of the electorate. the virus of misinformation is infecting the entire spectrum of political communication, casting doubt on even that information that is correct, and this misinformation can become lethal to democracy. i welcome the paris appeal, signed on 12 november and endorsed by 51 countries, including all european countries, as the manifesto to help ethical values and the rule of law prevail in the new digital society. the strategy proposed by the european commission to combat disinformation online will be put to the test in the next european elections. the truth is that we need a massive citizen coalition to make this strategy successful.
mr president, fake news is a reality of our times. they are another factor to be taken into account in the different variables of the electoral disputes that we obviously have to combat. but anyone who believes that the partisan reconfiguration we've seen in recent years, from russia to the united states to the european union, is all because of fake news is missing the point. some political forces, usually left-wing, have been colonizing the classical media for decades. since then, they've created all the narratives of political correctness: what can be said and what can't be said. and then they felt entitled to attack politicians who didn't follow their lead. political officeholders have become afraid to speak their minds, afraid of the media reaction. voters, for their part, have stopped hearing common-sense answers to concrete problems. and those who took advantage of the vacuum were all the extremists, also helped by fake news. anyone who does not understand this will hardly be able to help combat what is one of the worst phenomena of our times.
mr president, commissioners jourová and king, madam president-in-office of the council, ladies and gentlemen, in april of this year i asked the european commission about cybersecurity and safeguarding the integrity of european elections. no reply was given for many months, but finally on 12 september a package of measures was presented, for which i congratulate the european commission. in 2019, as already mentioned, there will be new elections to the european parliament. we all want to prevent any election manipulation. we are not naive and we know that there are many external forces, political, state, economic and military, who want to influence the electoral process, whether through fake news, the use of large amounts of data for electoral manipulation, or through funding extremist parties that want to destroy the european project. we all recall, as european commissioner jourová has already done, the shame of the facebook/cambridge analytica case and the strong evidence of russian interference in elections in europe, all of them using the digital world and the new information and communication technologies. it is therefore essential to safeguard the rights of european citizens in these elections. cybersecurity is more than ever a priority in the defence of the union's fundamental rights. it is essential that the commission and the member states take the necessary technical and organisational measures to manage the risks posed to the security of the network and information systems used in elections to the european parliament. in a europe contaminated by selfishness and populism, many will not look to any means to destroy the european project and to fight the europe of values and freedoms. we must act quickly for freedom.
mrs president, commissioner, persistent organic pollutants, of which perhaps the most media-worthy are ddt and dioxins, as the name suggests, persist in the environment, contaminating natural habitats and are highly toxic to us, humans, and other living beings. we know that most of these pollutants are, in fact, endocrine disruptors, which means that they affect the health and reproduction of a number of animal species, including our own. i therefore believe that strong measures are needed to eliminate these types of pollutants and to prevent ecotoxicity and bioaccumulation with exponential consequences for humans. as regards the permissible dust limits, we must impose the same rules on recycled products as we already impose on virgin products, without creating a duality of criteria or a difference in quality between products. in other words, encouraging the circular economy means ensuring consumer safety, especially as the amount of recycled source material is only likely to increase. we need to reassure citizens that they can rest assured that food, children's toys and everyday utensils are not contaminated with these highly dangerous substances.
mr president, by pulling russia out of this treaty, trump may in fact be playing putin's dangerous game, because the treaty has effectively reduced the risks of military escalation, operational miscalculation and has helped shift the mad logic from the cold war to that of dialogue between russia and the united states. even if there is a breach of the treaty by moscow and even if trump also aims to renegotiate with russia, and even indirectly aims at containing china's nuclear capability, the risks of escalation and catastrophe are tremendous, because russia today may be tempted to compensate for weakness in conventional forces by resorting to the use of its nuclear capabilities and in the middle is europe. it's insane not to see that our security and global security is at stake. the european union cannot stand still and fall. it must intervene in some way and try to negotiate with both of them so that we actually see a real reduction in these intermediate-range nuclear forces in russia, but also in all the others that have them.
president-in-office of the council, on behalf of the high representative / vice—president of the commission. – mr president, thank you for this important exchange. i think it has highlighted the importance of the preservation of the intermediate—range nuclear forces (inf) treaty for european and global security, as well as our common preoccupation with the increased threats to our security. disarmament, arms control and non—proliferation treaties and agreements like the treaty on the non—proliferation of nuclear weapons (npt) and inf constitute our first line of defence. dismantling such multilateral security arrangements and institutions is in nobody’s interest. because i was asked very explicitly, i would also like to answer the question as to whether i believe we can rescue the inf. yes, of course, i do believe that. we mustn’t give up on that, and must put all our efforts into the rescue of the inf. i also believe that debates like the one today in the european parliament remind people, including decision makers in moscow and washington, of the value of the inf treaty for european and global security and the stake that we in europe have in this treaty’s future. by the way, the inf is only one contribution to article 6 of the npt, so a lot more has to be done in the future. allegations that the russian federation has begun producing and developing new missiles which could be aimed at eu countries, and which are not in compliance with russia’s treaty obligations, deserve to be treated seriously and need to be resolved. this should happen through a responsible dialogue, including at the highest level. the stakes are simply too high.
member of the commission, on behalf of the vice—president of the commission / high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. – mr president, the ongoing caravans of migrants walking across central america and mexico turned public attention towards a silent exodus that has been ongoing for years. every country in the world has every right to protect its borders and every country in the world has the obligation to do so in full respect of human rights and the principle of non—refoulement, as well as the norms on international protection. this applies to europe and its member states as it applies to everyone else, including the united states. but beyond looking at the consequences, let me take this opportunity also to also look at the symptoms. every day, hundreds of people from honduras, guatemala and el salvador begin the journey to the united states. today migrants are more visible as part of caravans walking in broad daylight, accompanied by ngos and tv crews. they are all fleeing poverty and hunger, but fear seems to be the main driver of these migrations. the northern triangle countries are among the poorest in latin america with one third, or even half, of the population living on less than eur 5 a day. half of the unemployed are young people so it is hardly surprising that many of them decide to leave their country and look for a better future. natural disasters have also been a strong driver for migration from the region. today they are aggravated by the dire effects of climate change, in particular droughts and floods. all these factors combine and today the migrants are also fleeing death, violence and extortion. between 2010 and 2017, over 100 000 homicides were recorded in the northern triangle. it is probably no coincidence that the first of these recent caravans started in san pedro sula, which is one of the most violent cities in the world. the european union has been supporting countries in the region in a twofold approach, providing immediate relief and tackling the root causes. in terms of humanitarian assistance, the commission is implementing a program of eur 2.5 million to address the needs of people affected by violence in the northern triangle. concerning the caravan, the commission is currently looking into possibilities to respond to humanitarian needs, in particular regarding the protection of vulnerable children and women against trafficking. in honduras we are investing about eur 200 million in decent employment and food security, as well as strengthening the rule of law and access to justice. in guatemala support amounting to over eur 150 million is focused on food security, competitiveness security, security and human rights. in el salvador we are investing about eur 150 million. we support the national strategy to fight violence with a focus on prevention, including support to community organisations on the return of young people to the education system and on the construction of safe public spaces. the eu also funds social programmes such as ‘ciudad mujer’ an innovative approach to provide women with psychological and legal support and training for entrepreneurship. efforts to address security challenges at national level go hand in hand with reinforced regional cooperation against transnational crime and drug trafficking. regional cooperation is equally important for the development of the region, including the creation of economic opportunities. the association agreement between the eu and central america opens our markets to central american businesses. once fully ratified, the political and cooperation dimensions of the agreement will further reinforce our partnership. the eu is – and will remain – a reliable partner to the countries in the region in addressing the migration challenges, combining response to immediate needs with action on the root causes. migration is a global challenge that requires global solutions and global responsibility sharing. through international cooperation with our partners, we can turn migration from a common challenge into a shared opportunity for human development. i know that the parliament has always supported this approach so i thank you for this debate and look forward to hearing your views.
on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the so-called migrants' march which started on 13 october from the city of san pedro sula, and as the commissioner has said, one of the most dangerous cities in the world, in northern honduras, was joined by thousands of migrants from guatemala and el salvador. whole families follow on foot in caravans, with their eyes fixed on the united states. according to save the children, one in four of the members of this caravan are children and adolescents. lack of opportunity, extreme violence and endemic poverty in the northern triangle of central america have led these families to leave their homeland and embark on this journey that, while arduous, offers more security than solitary migration and avoids paying thousands of dollars to smugglers. many of these victims of economic misery and social exclusion also face persecution, forced recruitment and abduction by groups linked to organized crime, from local maras to transnational drug traffickers, a pattern that is unfortunately now being reproduced in nicaragua as well. these people are not criminals, as president trump never tires of repeating. they are victims of criminal organizations that, with violence and impunity, pretend to rule them, and they seek only to live in security and to find work that will enable them to support their families, as did thousands of europeans who fled to the united states in the 19th and 20th centuries to escape violence, discrimination and hunger. president trump, who in the 22 months of his presidency has demonized immigration, has promised to stop those now trying to reach his country under the guise of protecting american citizens from criminals and an impending invasion and has announced the deployment of troops to the southern united states and the militarization of the border. the chances of them being granted asylum are therefore slim, and many are at risk of being detained and separated from their families while awaiting court hearings that could end in a deportation order. this march of migrants and the one that started in the southern mexican city of tapachula last april, show that the hardening of asylum policies and mass deportations undertaken by the united states and mexico in recent years have failed and that their aim of deterring these flows and the announced cuts in economic aid to these countries will only aggravate the social chaos from which these people are fleeing. the european union should therefore continue to support regional integration efforts in central america and take the opportunity, within the framework of the association agreement with central america, to strengthen the political dialogue with the countries of the northern triangle on respect for human rights, the strengthening of the rule of law and the fight against drug trafficking and impunity. the union can also not fail to support the regional expansion of the un commission against impunity and to urge transit and destination countries to respect their international human rights obligations in all cross-border crossings. i would like to conclude, mr president, by referring to the right to life of all migrants, regardless of their status, respect for the principle of non-refoulement and the prohibition of arbitrary and collective expulsions.

the european commission's communication 'a clean planet for all', which is being debated today, is extremely timely and follows the presentation by the international energy agency of very pessimistic forecasts on the degree of global compliance with the paris agreement, which considered that even the best efforts of the eu may not be enough to contain the increase in emissions that is occurring, especially in asia. the communication stresses the importance of rapid implementation of the clean energy package and the ambitious completion of the ongoing institutional negotiations on clean mobility. it is important to underline that the communication 'a clean planet for all' aims to be a pillar of a long-term strategic vision that will enable the european union to make a sustainable and just transition to a prosperous, modern, competitive and decarbonised economy. the european union is now well equipped with the conceptual tools and is in the process of acquiring the legislative tools needed to aim for a position favourable to people, the economy and the environment in the energy transition process. the key, in an intergovernmental plan, is implementation. governments must follow up this legitimate european and citizen ambition.
the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for adoption. free capitalist competition in the single market has asymmetrical effects, which tend to accentuate economic and social divergence. on the one hand, the major economies of the european union and the eurozone, and their multinationals, which have colonized markets from the centre to the peripheries, are the winners. on the other hand, the economically weaker countries, exposed to unequal and destructive competition, whose markets have been colonized with strong disruption of the business fabric, are the losers. insufficient cohesion policy has never fully compensated for this divergent and asymmetrical effect. the deepening of the single market, on the other hand, has brought increasingly wider spheres of economic and social life under the control of the market, and is inseparable from liberalisation, privatisation and the attack on public services and the social functions of the state. the idyllic portrayal of the single market that the right and the social democrats repeatedly paint corresponds to only one side of the coin: that of the interests they defend. the workers and the people have no reason to see each other in this portrait.
mr president, the establishment of a new world order based on the deregulation and liberalization of international trade - that is, a new form of protectionism for the rich, a dictatorship of the multinationals, forcing competition between labour from different geographical origins in order to achieve the general devaluation of labour - was, and is, to a large extent, the aim of the world trade organization. the last few years have been marked by a substantial change in the balance of power at world level, by the rise and growing preponderance of the emerging economies and the breakdown of the relative hegemony of the united states and also of the european union and japan, which has dictated contradictions, constraints and blockages within the wto, with which these imperialist powers have not been able to deal in the way they wanted. the theses for reforming the wto to overcome blockages and contradictions thus emerge. the wto must be brought more into line with the interests for which it was set up. they are certainly not in the interests of the workers and the people. they are calling for another order, based on fair and regulated trade, complementarity, social progress and mutual benefit.

rapporteur. – madam president, let me begin by thanking commissioner malmström and the eu’s chief negotiator, mauro petriccione, for their intensive work on this agreement with japan and for our good cooperation. let me also thank the shadow rapporteurs. i believe that by working together and building compromises we have reached a very good outcome, so that we can have a meaningful vote tomorrow – not only on the agreement itself but also on a resolution in which parliament sends out important messages about trade, and calls on the parties to make the most of the progressive potential of this agreement. as standing rapporteur, let me begin by saying that we are discussing here much more than a trade agreement. yes, this is a historic moment. in front of us, we have the most important bilateral trade agreement ever concluded by the eu. two of the largest economies in the world, together representing nearly a third of the world’s gdp, will move closer to each other, eliminating a number of heavy tariffs as well as non—tariff barriers to trade. as a result, european products and services will get major new opportunities in the japanese market. acknowledging the successful outcome of the negotiations, all eu economic sectors are now speaking in favour of this agreement, including the agri—food sector, which will benefit from new export opportunities while protecting 205 geographical indications. no matter how relevant it may be from the economic point of view, however, this agreement is, above all, of major strategic importance. it gives us the chance to shape the global agenda at a time of rising protectionism, thus sending a clear and timely signal in favour of an open, fair and rules—based trading system. we should not miss this opportunity to promote our own values and standards in the asia—pacific region and in global trade. i would not be standing here as rapporteur supporting this eu—japan agreement if it were not a progressive trade agreement: an agreement clearly belonging to a new generation of trade agreements, in which sustainable development matters and environment, labour rights and consumer protection matter. of course, there is no such thing as a perfect agreement, but this agreement recognises the un sustainable development goals. it is the first trade agreement with a reference to the paris agreement on climate change and it fully respects public services and the right to regulate. moreover, it includes not only a commitment to pursue the ratification of the core international labour organisation (ilo) conventions but also a review clause for improving the enforceability of labour and environmental rules. japan, it is fair to say, has made substantial efforts, even before the entry into force of the agreement, and it is already discussing at inter—ministerial level the implementation of its sustainable development commitments. this should be warmly welcomed as a very good sign. parliament has closely followed these negotiations from the beginning. we were supportive but also demanding. last friday, the japanese parliament approved the agreement. tomorrow this parliament will have the final say. my position as rapporteur is that this trade agreement is economically balanced and of major strategic importance. furthermore, it clearly represents a step forward on sustainable development. therefore, i recommend that you support this important eu—japan economic partnership agreement and vote in favour of our resolution.

member of the commission. – madam president, i would like to thank the honourable members for this debate. as some of you said, every agreement is a compromise – you give and you take, and sometimes the end result is not exactly as you wanted but rather good. you may like this agreement or not, but you have to stick to the truth: there is nothing – and i repeat nothing – in this agreement forcing any country of the european union, or japan, to privatise public services. nor is there anything hindering any country of the european union, or japan, from taking back privatised services and renationalising them, as long as this is done in a non-discriminatory way. so stick to the truth. i also want to reject the claim that this is not transparent. over the past two years, there have been 40 meetings with eu governments and 15 meetings with members of this house, including the monitoring group. we have published negotiating texts. we have made available detailed reports of each negotiating round. we have had a lot of public meetings, i have attended several of them, and we put the whole text online in july 2017 when it was finalised. there are also many memos and fact sheets, including on a regional basis, and sometimes online, in which you can look at the content of this agreement. so please stick to the truth. on data adequacy, i also want to say that this house asked for clarifications. we have launched the process to adopt the final decision involving the opinion of our independent data protection authorities and, once that is finalised, which will be very soon, the eu-japan mutual adequacy decision would confirm our global lead in setting high standards for the benefit of our citizens. this is an agreement with a friend and an ally. japan is a strong democracy that shares our values, and together we stand for fair trade, free trade and sustainable trade, based on multilateral rules – and you all know that they are under question today. the agreement opens up fantastic possibilities for our goods, services and agri-food products, irish whiskey and many others, that can be sold to the benefit of japanese customers, and for cooperation in many areas beyond traditional trade. we have had a focus, jointly, on smes to try to help them in this regard. i have learned during these negotiations that we have much more in common with our japanese friends than maybe we thought a few years ago. i am confident that the trade agreement and the strategic partnership agreement will give us a framework for developing areas of cooperation that are already ongoing, and many others as well, and that we will set a high standard for the world. so, i hope all of you will feel, or at least most of you will feel, that you can vote in favour of this tomorrow so that it can enter into force early next year. thank you very much for a good debate.
rapporteur. – madam president, i am glad to see such wide support from the major political groups for this agreement and my draft resolution. i see it in part as a result of our fruitful cooperation. of course, i respect all the opinions expressed during the debate but i have to say that some of the arguments used against the agreement simply disregard its content – and that goes not only for this idea of privatisation of public services but also for the undermining of democratic basic rights, because the right to regulate is clearly protected in this agreement. others say that this agreement is only about free trade: on the contrary, this is a sound economic partnership agreement with a sustainable development chapter and important and binding environmental, labour and consumer-protection provisions. and some even say that this agreement will affect the wellbeing of japanese farmers – meaning, of course, that it is perhaps too good for european farmers. well, i’ve heard a lot of debate on other trade agreements because they might affect rural development and jobs in the agricultural sector, and now this agreement is deemed too good for european farmer! i think, on the contrary, that we have good reasons to be in favour of this agreement, because it is economically important, it is strategically important, it is a step forward regarding sustainable development, and it gives us a chance, as parliament, to send a clear message in favour of the progressive and full use of the agreement’s potential. let us not miss this opportunity tomorrow.

mr president, i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteurs. a commitment is a commitment, and we came here with a commitment. the commission's initial proposal again excluded the social sciences and the humanities. parliament has recovered them. it is impossible to intervene in societies we do not know, and i believe that the tragic events in strasbourg today have shown us this need. we guarantee the protection of the employment rights of researchers, such as maternity and paternity leave. we guarantee that there is no public funding for military research. we propose to strengthen convergence by also strengthening the widening programme. we've got more resources for small and medium-sized businesses. this commitment is not perfect and that is why we want to try tomorrow to eliminate the principle of innovation, which nobody knows very well what it is, except for big industry. anyway, it's been a busy few months. now, we need to close the negotiations and let the scientific community breathe. there are few examples of cooperation between the different institutions in this european union.
knowledge, innovation and technology will increasingly be the key instruments for the development of societies, their competitiveness and their assertion in an increasingly disruptive geopolitical context. it is important that the european parliament's draft report reinforces the need to secure funding for the horizon europe programme, funding of eur 120 billion in the period 2021-2027. the compatibility of the programme with the millennium development goals and the paris agreement commitments to combat global warming should be highlighted. 35% of horizon europe funding shall be used for measures with a favourable impact on climate action. the priority given to small and medium-sized enterprises (smes) is also key. eur 2.5 billion is allocated to incremental innovation in smes. the creation of conditions for greater participation of civil society in the monitoring of the programme and the increased focus on the social sciences are also decisive. the measures contained in the programme to promote convergence in access and reduce disparities between member states should be fully implemented and the programme should be simplified in administrative procedures, as proposed.
rapporteur. – mr president, as standing rapporteur i would like to react to this last-minute request by saying that i don’t see any reason whatsoever for a postponement. (applause)in fact, if we listen carefully to the request we see this is not really a request for postponement, because the reasoning is against the agreement as it is. now the agreement has been known about since december 2017. we heard all the stakeholders, we asked for clarifications from the japanese government and we got those clarifications. we have had a long discussion in this parliament, and the japanese parliament has already taken a decision. we are ready to do the same, and i say we should do it now, deciding on both the agreement and the progressive resolution that we have before us.
president-in-office of the council. – madam president, i would like to thank parliament for raising this very important topic and for giving me the chance to take the floor on behalf of the council. the timing of this debate is apt, given that international climate negotiators are currently gathered at cop24 in katowice in poland, discussing, inter alia, solidarity and ‘just transition’. the topic was immediately brought to the fore when, on 3 december 2018, at the leaders’ summit taking place during the conference, the polish presidency of cop24 announced the adoption of the solidarity and just transition silesia declaration. the european union joined the representatives of 45 states in giving its full support to the declaration, which remains open for others to join it. the silesia declaration underlines that a just transition of the workforce and the safeguarding and creation of sustainable employment and decent work are crucial to ensuring public support for long—term emission reductions. the eu’s support for the declaration, alongside that of its member states, is an important signal to the international community, showing our commitment to the implementation of the paris agreement. you are all aware of the enormous scale and urgency of the challenge that climate change poses to humankind. if left unconstrained, climate change will lead to large-scale and irreversible negative consequences for our environment and ecosystems, and for our economies. action against climate change is more urgent than ever. the paris agreement sets the goal of achieving net—zero greenhouse gas emissions globally in the second half of this century. the eu has long been at the forefront of the global response to climate change. it already has in place a legislative framework for reducing its greenhouse gas emissions up to 2030 and it has managed successfully to decouple greenhouse gas emissions from economic growth since the 1990s. however, we know that, even though they are among the most ambitious in the international context, the policies the eu has in place today will not be enough to contribute in a sufficient manner to the achievement of the paris agreement’s temperature goals. according to the paris agreement, all parties are to present long-term, low-greenhouse-gas-emission development strategies by 2020. on 28 november, responding to calls by the council and parliament to present a long-term strategy for the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions, the commission published a communication on a long—term strategy for the european union. it outlines a vision of the economic and societal transformations required, while also seeking to ensure that the transition to net—zero greenhouse gas emissions is socially fair. discussions of the long-term strategy within the council have already started and, before the end of our presidency, it will be debated by ministers responsible for energy at the council meeting scheduled for 19 december, and by those responsible for the environment on 20 december. the challenges posed by global climate change will require huge economic and societal transformation, which includes the continuing modernisation of european industries and economies with, notably, a transition to clean energy systems. just transition is an imperative that was included in the paris agreement to ensure that the consequences for the workforce of climate change measures in regions and communities which stand to be particularly affected by them are taken into account. some regions, globally and in europe, are more dependent than others on carbon-intensive industries or on coal mining. today, 41 regions in 12 eu countries are actively mining coal, and many face significant economic and social challenges. the transition towards low—carbon and climate—resilient economies and societies should ensure a just transition of the workforce, with the creation of decent work and quality jobs for all. while many sectors, cities and regions which are highly reliant on fossil fuels and high-emitting industries face undeniable challenges, the transition to climate—resilient economies also has the potential to create additional employment opportunities. according to a report published by the international labour organisation (ilo), this year, jobs created in renewable energy, building efficiency and other low—carbon industries can more than offset job losses in other sectors. however, policies will be needed to protect workers in the sectors where there will be fewer jobs in the future. the silesia declaration mentions the importance of the ilo guidelines for a just transition towards environmentally sustainable economies and societies for all, and emphasises that the un 2030 agenda and its sustainable development goals confirm the need to tackle environmental, social and economic problems in a coherent and creative manner. ensuring a just transition for the workers and communities concerned requires a participatory and progressive representative process of social dialogue, involving all social partners. just transition is finding its way into important pieces of eu legislation on climate and energy policy. with the inclusion of just transition as one of the investment priorities of the new modernisation fund, the revised emissions trading system (ets) directive will play an important role in supporting regions in 10 low-income member states, which are dependent on carbon-intensive industries, during their move towards a low—carbon economy. projects financed under the modernisation fund should support the redeployment, reskilling and upskilling of workers, education, jobseeking initiatives and start-ups, in dialogue with the social partners. in addition, the revised directive highlights the use of auction revenues by member states for the purpose of promoting skills formation and the reallocation of labour in order to contribute to a just transition to a low—carbon economy, in particular in regions most affected by the jobs transition. in both cases, member states are responsible for designing and implementing the relevant support schemes. it is to be noted that support is also available from a number of existing eu programmes and funding instruments. it is a very positive sign that the international community, governments, businesses and trade unions are increasingly paying attention to the just transition agenda. this is a conversation which needs to continue and to be as inclusive and participatory as possible.
member of the commission. – madam president, first of all i would like to thank the honourable members for tabling this important and topical debate. we can only have a clean transition if it is also a just transition, and we can only have a safer planet if we involve our workers and our citizens. this is a tale of two cities. while we are debating the issue today in strasbourg, the un climate conference – the cop24 – is taking place in katowice. katowice is a city in poland’s region of silesia, one of europe’s most coal-intensive regions and, as such, silesia perfectly illustrates the social economic challenges, but also opportunities, of transition to a low—carbon economy. this is why cop24 started last week by signing the silesia declaration on solidarity and just transition, a declaration endorsed by the european union as a whole and 20 eu member states individually, a declaration which stresses that environmental and social developments must go hand—in—hand. our european climate policy is the most forward-looking in the world. we aim to reduce our emissions by at least 40% by 2030, but the paris agreement requires us to look further into the future. two weeks ago, we published our proposal for the european union’s long-term strategy, a long—term strategy on the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions, and the proposal shows how europe can lead the way to climate neutrality by 2050 by investing in technological solutions, by empowering citizens, by taking action in industrial policy finance and research, and by ensuring social fairness. we need to decisively tackle climate change, and there are more benefits besides those clearly related to environment and health. already today the transformation is creating growth – green growth. in europe, the clean energy sector has created roughly four million jobs; investments into industrial modernisation, energy transformation, the circular economy and clean mobility will create more and better jobs. but change will come with its challenges and costs. these challenges might not be equally distributed between regions, sectors and between people. emission-heavy industries need to rethink their industrial processes, and if they don’t, there are job losses, social exclusion and depopulation in the region. the labour market will change. just like in the digital transition, some jobs will disappear, others will be created, and many jobs will change. workers in declining sectors need guidance and re-skilling, and we have to realise that some people won’t be able to upskill or re-skill – they need social protection, and that means adequate social protection. lastly, transition may affect most those who can afford it least – those who can’t invest in energy efficiency or low-carbon solutions. the success of the transition to the low-carbon economy depends on the support of our citizens; it should be seen not as a pet project of the elites. our policy should be progressive and should not punish the poorest. to avoid polarisation, to avoid inequality, we must base our policies on solidarity. social fairness should be part of all our climate policies, right from the start. we should not think about corrective actions post factum in order to remedy the consequences. instead, we should include social and fairness considerations at the outset from the moment we design our policy responses. also, therefore, we are putting a premium on investment. our proposal for the new mff has a strong focus on investment and innovation, with programmes such as horizon europe, the invest europe fund, the european regional and development fund, and the dedicated modernisation and innovation funds under the eu emissions trading system. we can anticipate skills needs and help workers change jobs by funding retraining and upskilling from the european social fund. our proposal for the post-2020 european globalisation adjustment fund helps workers who risk losing their jobs because of decarbonisation by funding retraining and activation measures. and with the european pillar of social rights, we direct policy attention to support for individual transition through investing in skills and lifelong learning and upskilling and re-skilling, but also through investment and adequate social protection. and we base our approach on partnership. for example, last year we launched the coal regions in transition initiative to facilitate green transition in 41 coal— and carbon—intensive regions located in 12 member states. climate change is a fact, and that’s why we are ambitious in our goals, and that’s why we are planning for the long term. the paris agreement is good for the planet and for the people, but it can only be made a reality and a success with the people on board.
on behalf of the alde group. madam president, commissioner, minister, a safer planet is one where violence, especially terrorism, especially the most extreme violence such as terrorism, is combated primarily by preventive political measures and not only by repressive police measures. a safer planet is one where we build more schools today for tomorrow, where we don't have to build more prisons. a safer planet is one where poverty is tackled with policies based on solidarity and this political imperative is not left to random economic growth and unpredictable expansion of markets. a safer planet is one where citizens trust their political representatives because they always respect their will and put the interests of their constituents above their own personal interests and personal comforts. a safer planet is one where citizens trust the veracity of the news they read and hear in the media, because they are sure that journalists produce information subordinated only to the dictates of truth and the public interest of the facts and not to the propaganda or advertising interests of news agency clients. a safer planet is one where citizens trust the judges who judge and decide in the courts, because they know that they do so always subordinated to the values of law and justice and not to hidden agendas of personal and/or corporate political interests. a safer planet will always have to be a fairer and less unequal planet. absolute equality will never be achieved among human beings, but the chasms that still separate men and women and especially the rich and the poor on this planet can already be narrowed. a safer planet, after all, is one on which we know how to preserve its biological diversity as well as its fragile environmental balances.
mr president, commissioner, madam minister, the social market economy model is based on the following triad: investment, labour and consumption. only investment creates employment, only employment increases consumption, only consumption increases production, and thus generates new investment, new jobs and new consumption. this is the trilogy of economic development and social progress. the state must not intervene in the market which thus functions, except to correct some of its deviations or excesses or to make up for some of its shortcomings. one of these deviations is the possibility for some economic operators to obtain high profits and incomes, often due only to the clever use of certain market constraints. this is precisely where the state can intervene through the mechanism of taxation. i therefore support the commission's proposal and welcome the work of the rapporteurs to create a tax on digital companies operating in the european union and making profits that are scandalously disproportionate to their investments and job creation.
mr president, the digital economy has been one of the most dynamic sectors of the economy and has provided enormous advantages to consumers in terms of the diversity, quality and price of the most varied services and products. however, it must be acknowledged that part of this dynamism and competitiveness has been based on a profound fiscal inequality vis-à-vis companies marketing equivalent products and services. the big companies in the digital economy have been living in a kind of virtual offshore that allows them to pay very little tax where they are based and none at all in the countries where they do business. i am in favour of introducing a digital tax for the sake of equality in the taxation of companies, which is indispensable for financing the social and economic functions of the states. i also hope that this will be the beginning of the movement towards taxation of all companies benefiting from tax-privileged schemes, whether in the european union or outside.
mr president, commissioner, in conjunction with other essential programmes in the field of strengthening the scientific and technological capacity of the european union, this european space programme is a strategic instrument for political and economic affirmation in the new global framework. the objective of the programme is very worthy: to integrate the various initiatives and implementation programmes and give them a new impetus, in order to ensure a competitive position of the european union in the space industries. it involves not only its reference companies, but also the knowledge centres and the network of small and medium-sized enterprises with high potential in this field. the development of a network of small and mediumsized enterprises throughout the union, which contribute to the european space strategy, is a condition for convergence which is worth investing in. the planned investment, as i said, of eur 16 billion for the european space programme, includes the budget of eur 9.7 billion for the galileo programme and eur 5.8 billion for the copernicus programme. the remaining 500 million is for initiatives in the field of government satellite communications and situational awareness in space. these are key initiatives for coordination, integration and monitoring, but they are clearly underfunded. the union needs all its programmes to be perceived by its citizens for the importance they can have in their daily lives. in the new technological framework, the scope of defending populations and vital interests is broader than established by the traditional reading of past conflicts and, therefore, to establish a rigid boundary between civilian purposes and the purposes of protection in case of conflict is an academic exercise. it is important that the emphasis is not on military uses and the report under consideration does not do that. however, european citizens should be able to feel that their economic security, their technological security and also their physical security are strengthened by these programmes. and this is also an added value of the integrated approach of this programme that we cannot afford to lose.
on behalf of the s&d group. madam president, commissioner, the success of the programme under discussion today will, as i have said, largely depend on the future of the european union as a shared space of peace, freedom and democracy. as the poet paul éluard said, the future is not what it used to be, and today, failing to include the technological frontier in areas such as those on which this programme is betting would mean an irreversible social, political and economic backwardness. we must therefore be profoundly rigorous and determined in defining the priorities, rules and methodologies to be applied. only a narrow and short-sighted view can call into question the funding which, although significant, is still small and results from the lack of overall ambition of the european commission's financial package. eur 2.7 billion to develop a powerful high-capacity computing network; eur 2.5 billion to strengthen skills in artificial intelligence; eur 2 billion to develop cybersecurity capabilities; eur 1.3 billion to ensure digital transformation in public administration, with citizen access and service delivery as a priority; eur 700 million to develop the necessary skills so that this information generates a significant volume of new high-quality employment opportunities, all of this together totalling eur 9.2 billion. sounds like a lot of money. however, when we compare this potential investment with the investment forecast by other major powers, namely the united states, china or japan, we realise that it is not an excessive investment after all, but a small investment. it is also because of this differential in financial effort that we must be particularly rigorous in implementing the programme. as the shadow rapporteur for the socialists and democrats, i would like to highlight the excellent work of collaboration with the rapporteur, whom i congratulate, and with the other groups, which has made it possible to give consistency and to translate into a report strong ideas, strong ideas which i would like to summarise. first, digital europe is not a programme to develop infrastructure, it needs infrastructure to succeed. and that is why it is very important to link this programme with other programmes such as the cohesion funds, horizon europe or the connecting europe facility. secondly, it is very important to set a strong ethical standard in the evaluation of projects funded by the programme. the report sets out important steps in this direction which must be further developed in the context of the european union as a whole. cooperation with third countries is another key issue and this report gives a balanced, flexible but robust answer, setting out the relationship benchmarks and clear criteria for defining winning partnerships. the convergence and inclusion dimension associated with the programme should also be ensured. therefore, the fact that each member state has at least one innovation and development hub within the european network is an important element in protecting the geographical and territorial balance. data protection is also a cross-cutting feature of the development matrix of the programme objectives. we are living, madam commissioner, madam president, in an age of energy and digital transition which is shaping a technological revolution. a revolution must always be guided by values and must have the interests of the citizens as the watermark of its development. the deepening inequalities, the perception of injustice and the fear of change have been driving citizens away from the european project. the digital europe programme must be a programme of connecting, not of excluding. this week, as the commissioner is well aware, the first 2 800 european municipalities were announced which will have guaranteed high-quality connections in their public spaces under the wifi4you initiative. 2 800 municipalities were selected from among 13 000 local territories. the digital europe programme must be able to feed into the new european network of services for citizens and advocacy for citizens; it has to be the embryo of a differentiated digital identity based on our common values. that will also be the measure of your success or failure. we all, who believe in this project of peace and freedom that is the european union, have to cooperate to make a lot happen with the resources we have, mobilizing civil society and european businesses for this key strategic challenge, the challenge, after all, of putting the technological and digital revolution at the service of people, the decentralization of the planet, justice and the quality of life.
mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the european union is an area of freedom and security which must be based on full respect for fundamental rights. the reports coming from the croatian-bosnian border are therefore worrying. it is true that each member state determines who is allowed to enter or not to enter its territory, but in an area of free movement the external borders of each state become, in reality, common external borders. so we share a set of rules on how we carry out border controls, the so-called schengen rules, how we process asylum applications and even how we process the return of people. we must clearly condemn the violation of these rules, but we must also come up with solutions, and that is why i call on the european commission to make use of the schengen evaluation mechanism so that it can identify and recommend solutions as a matter of urgency. this does not mean that we favour an open door policy; it simply means that not everything is worth controlling borders, let alone questioning basic principles of humanity. we are faced with another symptom of this greater disease, namely the inaction of the member states, their inability to accept reforms which this parliament has long advocated. we need an effective return policy, a functioning common european asylum system with a strengthened european agency and a robust and effective border agency. once again, mr. president, the weakest are paying for the weakness of the strongest.
author. – mr president, the lawyer nasrin sotoudeh was in jail when we awarded her the 2012 sakharov prize and she was never able to come to meet us because she has now been thrown in jail for several years in iran for her indefatigable advocacy for human rights and women’s rights. her husband, reza khandan, because he dared to protest publicly against her imprisonment, was also arrested and accused of spreading propaganda against the regime and promoting the practice of appearing in public without a veil. such ridiculous charges tell us everything about the tragedy in which iran is ensnared. on the one hand, a vibrant people, proud of its civilisation and full of potential; on the other, an archaic regime that needs to impose brutal repression on the people. the new law on drugs has been positive, sparing the death penalty for thousands of iranian citizens. but torture, unfair trials, the discrimination of minorities and religious persecution... all of that compounds the terrible repressive framework against which the citizens of iran increasingly bravely resist and denounce. when i returned to iran last february in a delegation of the european parliament, i could sense the people were much more vocal, and therefore we call for the release of all the political prisoners, all the human rights campaigners like nasrin sotoudeh. the more we feel attached to the joint comprehensive plan of action (jcpoa), which is also about human rights, the more we need to raise human rights and indeed demand that this action ends.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group. mr president, egypt is certainly an important partner of the european union and a neighbouring country with which we have many centuries of common history. i've been working with the egyptian authorities since 2009, and that's why everything we have to say must be said very frankly and face to face. there are trade agreements between the european union and egypt. we recognize the sovereignty of the egyptian people, but we cannot continue to turn a blind eye to the systematic violation of human rights in the name of commercial interests. what we are seeing in egypt is a closing of the political space, severe limitations on the freedoms of expression, opinion, association. there can be no impunity for human rights in egypt, in the european union or anywhere else in the world. in the interests of good relations, we cannot continue to turn a blind eye or a deaf ear to all the appeals and complaints that come to us from citizens and civil society organisations, which i believe are only the tip of the iceberg. respect for human rights by the egyptian authorities is a favour first of all to the egyptian people themselves.
